where
S_{q}
is an orthogonal polar factor of

\big[\widehat{P}_{\ell+j}^{(i)}\big]^{\T}\big[\sum_{p\in\mathbb{I}_{q}}\phi_{\ell+p}(T_{1}(P))D_{p}\big]
for

for

and for each

with
m_{j}\geq 2
,

is implicitly determined by

\widetilde{P}_{\ell+j}^{(i+1)}=\widehat{P}_{\ell+j}^{(i)}S_{j}

where
S_{j}
is an orthogonal polar factor of
\big[\widehat{P}_{\ell+j}^{(i)}\big]^{\T}D_{j}
for

1\leq j\leq t
.

*
\phi_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\partial\phi(\boldsymbol{x})/\partial x_{j}
for
\boldsymbol{x}=[x_{j}]
.

Theorem
5.2
improves Theorem
5.1
when it comes to
T=T_{1a}
:
the objective value
increases additional
\delta
more. We notice, by Lemma
B.11
,
that
\delta\geq 0
always and it is strict
unless
\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)\succeq 0
.
Also note
\widetilde{P}
satisfies

\widetilde{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)\succeq 0
.
As a result of Theorem
5.2
, along the same line of the proof of Theorem
3.1
,
we have another necessary condition on a maximizer
P_{*}
of (
5.1
) with
T=T_{1a}

in Corollary
5.1
, beyond the ones in
Theorem
3.1
.

Corollary 5.1
.

Suppose
A_{i}\succeq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
and that
\phi
is convex and differentiable with

\phi_{j}(\boldsymbol{x})\geq 0
for
1\leq j\leq\ell
. If
P_{*}
is a maximizer of (
5.1
) with
T=T_{1a}
, then we
have not only (
2.3
) for
P=P_{*}
with
\Lambda=\Lambda_{*}:=P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P_{*})\succeq 0

but also
P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P_{*})\succeq 0
.

In Table
3
, we list conditions on partial derivatives
\phi_{j}

and the best choices of
Q_{i}
at Line 4 of Algorithm
1

that, when it is not
I_{k}
, can increase the objective value even more per SCF iterative step than
the NPDo Ansatz
suggests.

Having said that, we notice that
P^{(i)}
as
i
varies may belong to different subsets
\mathbb{P}
of

{\rm St}(k,n)
. For example, with
T=T_{1a}
, if
Q_{i}
is calculated according to Theorem
5.2
, i.e.,
Q_{i}
is an orthogonal polar factor of
\big[\widehat{P}^{(i)}\big]^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P^{(i)})
,
then
P^{(i+1)}\in\mathbb{P}_{i}:=\{X\in{\rm St}(k,n)\,:\,X^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P^{(i)})\succeq 0\}
that varies from one iterative step to the next. Eventually,

\mathbb{P}_{i}
approaches
\mathbb{P}_{*}:=\{X\in{\rm St}(k,n)\,:\,X^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P_{*})\succeq 0\}
by
Corollary
5.1
.
Similar comments can be said about
T_{1}
with (
5.7
) or (
5.8
) that we discussed
towards the end of Example
5.1
.
Numerically, such variations in
\mathbb{P}
does not pose any problem for Algorithm
1

to compute an approximate maximizer for the maximization problem (
5.1
).

Remark 5.1
.

We conclude this section by commenting on the applicability of the results of this section to the objective functions
in Table
1
via convex compositions of atomic functions for NPDo. Essentially our results are applicable to
all of those that are not in the quotient form, i.e., SEP, MBSub, SumCT, TrCP, UMDS, and DFT, assuming that matrices
A
and
A_{i}

are positive semidefinite. SEP is simply about the atomic function
\tr(P^{\T}AP)
.
For OLDA and SumTR, the corresponding composing functions
\phi
are

x_{2}/x_{1}
and
x_{2}/x_{1}+x_{3}
, respectively, but both are non-convex.
The composing function for OCCA is
\phi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})=x_{2}/\sqrt{x_{1}}
where
\boldsymbol{x}=[x_{1},x_{2}]^{\T}
, which is not convex but whose square

\phi(\boldsymbol{x}):=[\phi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})]^{2}=x_{2}^{2}/x_{1}
is convex for
x_{2}\geq 0
and
x_{1}>0
. Unfortunately,
the atomic function associated with
x_{1}
is
\tr(P^{\T}BP)
, for which

\phi_{1}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\partial\phi(\boldsymbol{x})/\partial x_{1}=-(x_{2}/x_{1})^{2}\leq 0
, violating the conditions of Theorems
5.1

and
5.2
. A similar argument applies to
\Theta
TR.
So the NPDo approach does not work for OLDA, OCCA, and
\Theta
TR. But, fortunately, the NEPv approach next will.

Part II
The NEPv Approach

6
The NEPv Framework

There are cases for which

\mathscr{H}(P):=\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}\equiv H(P)P

(6.1)

for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
(or even
\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
), where
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a symmetric matrix-valued function
dependent of
P
, e.g., for

f(P)=\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP)}{\tr(P^{\T}BP)}+\tr(P^{\T}CP)
from the sum of trace ratios (SumTR)
[
75
,
76
]
,
which includes SEP and OLDA as special cases,

\mathscr{H}(P)=2\left[\frac{1}{\tr(P^{\T}BP)}\left(A-\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP)}{\tr(P^{\T}BP)}\,B\right)+C\right]P\equiv H(P)P

(6.2)

for
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, where
H(P)
is easily identified.
In fact, Lu and Li
[
46
, Lemma 2.1]
show that
(
6.1
) always hold for some symmetric and right-unitarily invariant
H(P)
if
f
is right-unitarily invariant.

As a result of (
6.1
), the KKT condition (
2.3
) is an NEPv:

H(P)P=P\Omega,\,\,P\in{\rm St}(k,n).

(6.3)

Necessarily
\Omega=P^{\T}H(P)P\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is symmetric.

But not all
\mathscr{H}(P)={\partial f(P)}/{\partial P}
take the form
H(P)P
, and in the latter,
we can still construct some
H(P)
to turn the KKT condition (
2.3
) equivalently into an NEPv in the form of
(
6.3
) under some mild condition.
For example, for

f(P)=\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}}

of the
\theta
-trace ratio problem (
\Theta
TR)
which includes OCCA and the MAXBET subproblem as special cases,
the authors of
[
67
]
used

H(P)=\frac{2}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}}\left(A+\frac{DP^{\T}+PD^{\T}}{2}-\theta\,\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)}{\tr(P^{\T}BP)}\,B\right).

(6.4)

In general, we can always take

H(P):=[\mathscr{H}(P)]P^{\T}+P[\mathscr{H}(P)]^{\T}=\left[\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}\right]P^{\T}+P\left[\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}\right]^{\T}.

(6.5)

In the case when
\mathscr{H}(P)\equiv H_{0}(P)P
, this
H(P)
becomes
2\,H_{0}(P)
for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
.

Why
H(P)
in (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) work for
\Theta
TR and
in general, respectively, can be best explained by the next theorem.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a symmetric matrix-valued function satisfying

H(P)P-\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}=P\,\mathscr{M}(P)\quad\mbox{for $P\in{\rm St}(k,n)$},

(6.6)

where
\mathscr{M}(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is some matrix-valued function.

P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
is a solution to the KKT condition (
2.3
) if and only if
it is a solution to NEPv (
6.3
)
and
\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric.

Proof.

If
P
is a solution to the KKT condition (
2.3
), i.e.,

\mathscr{H}(P)=P\Lambda
,
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, and
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\T}
.
Then, by (
6.6
),

H(P)P=P\Lambda+P\mathscr{M}(P)=P(\Lambda+\mathscr{M}(P))=:P\Omega,

where
\Omega=\Lambda+\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric because alternatively
\Omega=P^{\T}H(P)P
which is symmetric, and hence
\mathscr{M}(P)=\Omega-\Lambda
is also symmetric.
On the other hand, if
P
is a solution to NEPv (
6.3
) such that
\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric, then again
by (
6.6
)

\mathscr{H}(P)=P\Omega-P\mathscr{M}(P)=P\big(\Omega-\mathscr{M}(P)\big)=:P\Lambda,

where
\Lambda=\Omega-\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric because
\mathscr{M}(P)
is assumed symmetric.
∎

According to Theorem
6.1
, to solve the KKT condition (
2.3
) via solving
NEPv (
6.3
) with an
H(P)
that satisfies (
6.6
), we need to limit the solutions to those of the NEPv such that

\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric.
Return to the concrete
H(P)
given by (
6.4
) and (
6.5
). It can be
verified that, for
H(P)
in (
6.4
) for
\Theta
TR,

H(P)P-\mathscr{H}(P)=P\left(\frac{1}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}}\,{D^{\T}P}\right),

and hence any solution to the resulting NEPv (
6.3
) such that
D^{\T}P
is symmetric is a KKT
point of
\Theta
TR and vice versa.

Similarly, for
H(P)
in (
6.5
) in general,

H(P)P-\mathscr{H}(P)=P([\mathscr{H}(P)]^{\T}P)

and hence any solution to the resulting NEPv (
6.3
) such that
[\mathscr{H}(P)]^{\T}P
is symmetric is a KKT
point and vice versa.

We note that (
6.6
) is a guiding equation for
H(P)
, and satisfying (
6.6
)
yields a candidate
H(P)
and the resulting NEPv (
6.3
).
In general, given
\mathscr{H}(P)
, there are infinitely many
H(P)

that satisfy (
6.6
).

Our goal in this part is still the same as
in Part I, namely establishing conditions under which SCF (
1.6
) on NEPv (
6.3
) is provably convergent,
except that the conditions will be imposed on
H(P)
, instead of
\mathscr{H}(P)
earlier.
The developments in this section follow the lines of
[
67
,
77
]
, but in more abstract terms.

6.1
The NEPv Ansatz

The successes of the NEPv approach used in
[
67
,
77
]
for solving
OCCA and
\Theta
TR relies on certain monotonicity lemmas which inspire us to make the following
ansatz to build our framework upon. It also requires a sufficiently inclusive subset
\mathbb{P}
of

{\rm St}(k,n)
as in the NPDo framework in Part I.

The NEPv Ansatz.

For function
f
defined in some neighborhood
{\rm St}_{\delta}(k,n)
of the Stiefel manifold
{\rm St}(k,n)
,
there is a
symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that for

\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n),\,P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)
,
if

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})\geq\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)+\eta\quad\mbox{{for some $\eta\in\mathbb{R}$}},

(6.7)

then
there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such that
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
and

f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\omega\eta
,
where
\omega
is some positive constant, independent of
P
and
\widehat{P}
.

For any
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, there is always

\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
such that (
6.7
) holds with some
\eta>0
, unless for that given
P
,

H(P)P=P\Omega
holds and the eigenvalues of
\Omega
consist of the
k
largest eigenvalues of
H(P)
.
In fact, we can take

\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
to be an orthonormal basis matrix of the eigenspace of
H(P)

associated with its
k
largest eigenvalues, which also maximizes

\tr(X^{\T}H(P)X)
over
X\in{\rm St}(k,n)

[
20
]
.
Hence, for the purpose of solving (
1.1
),
we may relax the ansatz to
\eta\geq 0
only.
In general, it is the desirable aim,
f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\omega\eta
, in
the NEPv Ansatz

that needs to be verified before the general theory of this section can be applied.
Below we will use the same objective function in Example
3.1
to rationalize this ansatz.

Example 6.1
.

Consider
f(P)=\tr(P^{\T}AP)+\tr((P^{\T}D)^{2})

where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is symmetric and

D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. Note now no longer
A
is required to be positive semidefinite as it had to be in Example
3.1
.
Since
\mathscr{H}(P)=2AP+2DP^{\T}D
, no longer there exists a symmetric
H(P)
such that (
6.1
) holds.
Our discussion above leads us to use

H(P)=2A+2(DP^{\T}DP^{\T}+PD^{\T}PD^{\T})
for which

H(P)P-\mathscr{H}(P)=P\big[2(D^{\T}P)^{2}\big])\quad\mbox{for}\quad P\in{\rm St}(k,n),

satisfying (
6.6
).
To achieve equivalency between the KKT condition (
2.3
) and NEPv (
6.3
), according to
Theorem
6.1
we should limit the scope to those
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
such that
(D^{\T}P)^{2}
is symmetric.
Actually we will further limit the scope to
P\in\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
.
Suppose now that
(
6.7
) holds for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
and
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, or equivalently,

2\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}A\widehat{P})+4\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})\geq 2\tr(P^{\T}AP)+4\tr((P^{\T}D)^{2})+\eta.

(6.8)

Next let
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
be an orthogonal polar factor
of
\widehat{P}^{\T}D
and let
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
. We find that

\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}A\widetilde{P})=\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}A\widehat{P})
, but it remains to break the second term in the left-hand side of
(
6.8
) apart so that
P
and
\widehat{P}
are detached. For that purpose, we note

\displaystyle 2\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{\tr}\qquad(\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{lm:maxtrace}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}D\|_{\tr}\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}D\|_{\tr}\qquad(\mbox{since $\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}\leq 1$})

\displaystyle\leq\tr((Q^{\T}\widehat{P}^{\T}D)^{2})+\tr((P^{\T}D)^{2})\qquad(\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{lm:vN-tr-ineq-ext1}})

\displaystyle=\tr((\widetilde{P}^{\T}D)^{2})+\tr((P^{\T}D)^{2}).

(6.9)

Combine (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) to get

f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\eta/2
upon noticing
\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}A\widetilde{P})=\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}A\widehat{P})
.
As in Example
3.1
, we observe the critical conditions:

P^{\T}D\succeq 0
and
\widetilde{P}^{\T}D\succeq 0
(i.e.,
P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{P}
), that we used to derive (
6.9
),
where
\widetilde{P}^{\T}D=Q^{\T}\widehat{P}^{\T}D\succeq 0
is again made possible by the chosen
Q
.

Remark 6.1
.

A few comments on
the NEPv Ansatz
are in order.

(i)

The NEPv Ansatz
critically involves a symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
that has to be constructed. In
Theorem
6.1
, we provide a guiding equation (
6.6
)
for the purpose
so that the KKT condition
(
2.3
) and NEPv (
6.3
) are equivalent as far as solving the associated optimization problem
(
1.1
) is concerned. It is fulfilled naturally when
\mathscr{H}(P)\equiv H(P)P
for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
exactly (e.g., for OLDA, SumTR, TrCP, UMDS, and DFT), but
at other times, we will have to construct
H(P)
individually based on the particularity of
\mathscr{H}

as in
[
77
]
for OCCA,
[
67
]
for
\Theta
TR,
[
74
]

for MAXBET and Example
6.1
, or we simply use the generic (
6.5
).
The ansatz does not demand any explicit association of
H(P)
with
\mathscr{H}(P)
, but conceivably
they should be highly related, such as the relation imposed by (
6.6
).

(ii)

One may argue that the ansatz might be made unnecessarily complicated. After all

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})=\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})
. Should we get rid of
\widehat{P}
in the ansatz altogether?
One possibility is to require that

\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})\geq\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)+\eta
for
P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{P}

implies
f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\omega\eta
.

(6.10)

This is a stronger version, however,
assuming
that for any
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such that
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
.
Here is why.
Suppose that (
6.10
) holds. Given
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n),\,P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)
,
let
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
for some
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
. If (
6.7
) holds, then

\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})=\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})\geq\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)+\eta,

which, under (
6.10
), yields
f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\omega\eta
, proving the desired inequality
of
the NEPv Ansatz
.

(iii)

When
f(P)
is right-unitarily invariant,
H(P)
always exists such that (
6.1
) holds and can be taken to be right-unitarily invariant, too
[
46
, Lemma 2.1]
.
In such a case, the ansatz can be simplified to:
Q=I_{k}
and
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}
always because
f(\widehat{P})=f(\widetilde{P})

regardless of
Q
. Also often
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
.

(iv)

Introducing a subset
\mathbb{P}
of
{\rm St}(k,n)
and judiciously choosing
Q

are for generality to deal with the case when
f(P)

is not right-unitarily invariant, e.g., the one in Example
6.1
and those in Table
1
in section
1
that involve

D
or
D_{i}
. Suitable
Q
can also increase the objective value more than
\omega\eta
.
For example, for
\Theta
TR with
H(P)
given
by (
6.4
),
the NEPv Ansatz
holds with taking

Q
to be an orthogonal polar factor of
\widehat{P}^{\T}D
. In fact, along the line of the proof of

[
67
, Lemma 2.1]
,

assuming
B\succeq 0
,
\rank(B)>n-k
, and
\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)\geq 0
in the case of
0<\theta<1
but otherwise
no need to impose nonnegativity on
\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)
for
\theta\in\{0,1\}
,

we can improve the conclusion of
[
67
, Theorem 2.2]
to (in the current notation)

\displaystyle f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{s_{k}(B)}{S_{k}(B)}\right)^{\theta}\,\Big[\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})-\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)\Big]

\displaystyle+[S_{k}(B)]^{-\theta}\,\Big[\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}-\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})\Big],

(6.11)

where
0\leq\theta\leq 1
.
The last term is contributed by
the selection of
Q
as described.
A proof of (
6.11
) is given in Appendix
F
.

The comments in Remark
3.1
(ii) on
\mathbb{P}
apply here, too.

(v)

It is tempting to stipulate
f(\widehat{P})\geq f(P)+\omega\eta
, but that is either false or just hard to prove
for the one in Example
6.1
and some of those in Table
1
that involve
D
. Often in our algorithms to solve (
1.1
) iteratively, with
P
being
the current approximate maximizer, assuming
the NEPv Ansatz
, we naturally
compute
\widehat{P}
that maximizes
\tr(X^{\T}H(P)X)
over
X\in{\rm St}(k,n)
. With that
\widehat{P}
, settling
whether
f(\widehat{P})\geq f(P)+\omega\eta
or not can be a hard task, as

in Example
6.1

where the objective function involves

\tr((P^{\T}D)^{2})
.

Table 4:
The NEPv Ansatz
on objective functions in Table
1

\mathbb{P}

A

[

]
,
[

, p.248]

(
6.4
) with
\theta=1
,

,

)

,

,

) for case

) for case
\theta=1

,

\theta=0

)

]

\phi

{\rm St}(k,n)

convex
\phi

for
\boldsymbol{x}=[x_{i}]
.
