\theta=0
becomes MBSub.

As to the validity of
the NEPv Ansatz
on the objective functions in Table
1
,
it holds for all, except SumTR, under mild conditions on the constant matrices and function
\phi
.
Table
4

provides the details on
H(P)
and conditions under which

the NEPv Ansatz
holds, where the last column refers to places for justifications.
We leave
\mathbb{P}
unspecified for SumCT but refer it to
[
66
]
because it is more complicated to fit the space in the table.
In fact, it is required that
each
P_{i}
falls in
\{X\in{\rm St}(k_{i},n)\,:\,X^{\T}D_{i}\succeq 0\}
. Later in subsection
8.2
we will argue that for SumCT it would be more efficient
to go for the NPDo approach in Part I.
For SumTR with
H(P)
given by
(
6.2
),
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
and
Q=I_{k}
and
the NEPv Ansatz
does not hold.
This can be drawn from the counterexample,
[
76
, Example 4.1]
, for which SCF diverges,
but later we will show, under
the NEPv Ansatz
, SCF is guaranteed to converge!

Comparing Table
2
for NPDo with Table
4
for NEPv here, we find that,
among those in Table
1
,

the NEPv Ansatz
provably holds for three more of them, which are OLDA, OCCA, and
\Theta
TR (all involving ratios), than
the NPDo Ansatz
does. This observation that
the NEPv Ansatz
is satisfied more often than

the NPDo Ansatz
among those in Table
1
is not an accident.
In fact
the NPDo Ansatz
is stronger than
the NEPv Ansatz
with
the generic
H(P)
in (
6.5
),
as shown by the next theorem.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let function
f
be defined on some neighborhood
{\rm St}_{\delta}(k,n)
of
{\rm St}(k,n)
and let
H(P)
be as in (
6.5
).
Then
the NPDo Ansatz
implies
the NEPv Ansatz
.

Proof.

Suppose that
the NPDo Ansatz
holds.
Given
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n),\,P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)
such that (
6.7
) holds,
let
W\in{\rm St}(k,k)
be an orthogonal polar factor of
\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)
, and set
\check{P}=\widehat{P}W_{1}
.
Then
\check{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)=W_{1}^{\T}[\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)]\succeq 0
, and thus by Lemma
B.9

\tr(\check{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P))=\|\check{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\|_{\tr}=\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\|_{\tr}\geq\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)).

(6.12)

Recalling (
6.5
), we have

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{\tr}\qquad(\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{lm:maxtrace}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\|_{\tr}\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\|_{\tr}\qquad(\mbox{since $\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}\leq 1$})

\displaystyle\leq 2\tr(\check{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)).\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:NPDo>=NEPv:pf-1}})

Now noticing that
\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)=2\tr(P^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P))
, we get from inequality (
6.7
) that

\tr(\check{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P))\geq\tr(P^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P))+\eta/2.

By
the NPDo Ansatz
, there exists
W_{2}\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such that
\widetilde{P}=\check{P}W_{2}=\widehat{P}(W_{1}W_{2})\in\mathbb{P}
and

f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+(\omega/2)\eta
, verifying
the NEPv Ansatz
.
∎

The first immediate consequence of
the NEPv Ansatz
is the following theorem that provides a characterization of
the maximizers of the associated optimization problem (
1.1
).

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
P_{*}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
be a maximizer of (
1.1
).
Suppose that
the NEPv Ansatz
holds and
P_{*}\in\mathbb{P}
.
Then NEPv
(
6.3
) holds for
P=P_{*}
and the eigenvalues of
\Omega=\Omega_{*}:=P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*}

consist of the first
k
largest eigenvalues of
H(P_{*})
.

Proof.

Consider

\max_{P\in{\rm St}(k,n)}\tr(P^{\T}H(P_{*})P).

(6.13)

We claim
P_{*}
is a maximizer of (
6.13
); otherwise there would be some

\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
such that

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P_{*})\widehat{P})\geq\tr(P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*})+\eta

for some
\eta>0
. Invoking
the NEPv Ansatz
, we can find
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
such that

f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P_{*})+\omega\eta>f(P_{*})
, contradicting that
P_{*}
is a maximizer.
Thus
P_{*}
is a maximizer of (
6.13
) whose KKT condition is

H(P_{*})P=P\Omega
which
P_{*}
will have to satisfy, i.e.,

H(P_{*})P_{*}=P_{*}\Omega_{*}
, where
\Omega_{*}=P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*}
whose eigenvalues consists of
the
k
largest ones of
H(P_{*})
.

∎

6.2
SCF Iteration and Convergence

The second immediate consequence of
the NEPv Ansatz
is the global convergence of an SCF iteration
for solving optimization problem (
1.1
) as outlined in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

NEPvSCF: NEPv (
6.3
) solved by SCF

0:

Symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)
satisfying
the NEPv Ansatz
,
P^{(0)}\in\mathbb{P}
;

0:

an approximate maximizer of (
1.1
).

1:

for

i=0,1,\ldots
until convergence
do

2:

compute
H_{i}=H(P^{(i)})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
;

3:

solve SEP
H_{i}\widehat{P}^{(i)}=\widehat{P}^{(i)}\Omega_{i}
for
\widehat{P}^{(i)}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
,
an orthonormal basis matrix of the eigenspace associated with the first
k
largest eigenvalues of
H_{i}
;

4:

calculate
Q_{i}\in{\rm St}(k,k)
and let
P^{(i+1)}=\widehat{P}^{(i)}Q_{i}\in\mathbb{P}
, according to
the NEPv Ansatz
;

5:

end

for

6:

return
the last
P^{(i)}
.

This algorithm is similar to
[
77
, Algorithm 2]
,
[
67
, Algorithm 4.1]
,
but the latter two have more details that are dictated by
the particularity of
f
there. A reasonable stopping criterion at Line 1 is

\varepsilon_{\NEPv}:=\frac{\|H(P)P-P[P^{\T}H(P)P]\|_{\F}}{\xi}\leq\epsilon,

(6.14)

where
\epsilon
is a given tolerance, and
\xi
is some normalization quantity that should be
designed according to the underlying
H(P)
, but generically,
\xi=\|H(P)\|_{\F}
, or any reasonable estimate of it,
should work well.

The cost of a full eigendecomposition of
H_{i}
at Line 3 is
4n^{3}/3
flops
[
24
, p.463]
which is too expensive
for large or even modest
n
, since we have to do it at every SCF iterative step.
Fortunately, we do not need the full eigendecomposition but the top
k
eigenvalues and their associate eigenvectors.
Since
k
is usually small such as a few tens or smaller, a better option is some iterative methods geared for
extreme eigenpairs
[
23
,
33
,
41
,
53
]
.

Furthermore, as far as always moving the objective value up is concerned,
it suffices to calculate
\widehat{P}^{(i)}
just well enough such that

\tr([\widehat{P}^{(i)}]^{\T}H_{i}\widehat{P}^{(i)})>\tr([P^{(i)}]^{\T}H_{i}P^{(i)})
. This observation can become very useful when
the
k
th and
(k+1)
st eigenvalues of
H_{i}
are very close, in which case convergence to the
k
th eigenvector by
an iterative method is often very slow.

At Line 4 it refers to
the NEPv Ansatz
for the calculation of
Q_{i}
.
Exactly how it is computed depends on the structure of
f
at hand. We commented on the similar issue for Algorithm
1
earlier. In the case of Example
6.1
,
Q_{i}
is taken to be
an orthogonal polar factor of
(\widehat{P}^{(i)})^{\T}D
to make
P^{(i+1)}\in{\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
.

In Algorithm
1
, we explicitly state that it is for
H(P)
that satisfies
the NEPv Ansatz
, without which
we cannot guarantee convergence as stated in the theorems in the rest of this section, but numerically the body of
the algorithm can still be implemented. There is a level-shifting technique that can help achieve local convergence

[
4
,
46
]
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Suppose that
the NEPv Ansatz
holds, and let the sequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
be generated by Algorithm
1
.
The following statements hold.

(a)

The sequence
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is monotonically increasing and convergent.

(b)

Any accumulation point
P_{*}
of the sequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
satisfies
the necessary conditions in Theorem
6.3
for a global maximizer, i.e.,
(
6.3
) holds for
P=P_{*}
and the eigenvalues of
\Omega_{*}=P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*}

consist of the first
k
largest eigenvalues of
H(P_{*})
.
Furthermore in the case when
H(P)

satisfies (
6.6
), if
\mathscr{M}(P_{*})
is symmetric, then
P_{*}
is a KKT point.

(c)

We have two convergent series

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\delta_{i}\,\big\|\sin\Theta\big({\cal R}(P^{(i+1)}),{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\big)\big\|_{\F}^{2}

\displaystyle<\infty,

(6.15a)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\delta_{i}\,\frac{\big\|H(P^{(i)})P^{(i)}-P^{(i)}\Lambda_{i}\big\|_{\F}^{2}}{\big\|H(P^{(i)})\big\|_{\F}^{2}}

\displaystyle<\infty,

(6.15b)

where
\delta_{i}=\lambda_{k}(H(P^{(i)}))-\lambda_{k+1}(H(P^{(i)}))
and

\Lambda_{i}=[P^{(i)}]^{\T}H(P^{(i)})P^{(i)}
.

Proof.

See appendix
D
.
∎

As a corollary of Theorem
6.4
(b), we establish a sufficient condition for NEPv (
6.3
) to have a solution.

Corollary 6.1
.

Under
the NEPv Ansatz
,
NEPv (
6.3
) is solvable, i.e., there exists
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
such that
(
6.3
) holds and
the eigenvalues of
\Omega=P^{\T}H(P)P
are the
k
largest ones of
H(P)
.

As a corollary of Theorem
6.4
(c),
if
\delta_{i}=\lambda_{k}(H(P^{(i)}))-\lambda_{k+1}(H(P^{(i)}))
is eventually bounded below away from
0
uniformly, then

\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{\big\|H(P^{(i)})P^{(i)}-P^{(i)}\Lambda_{i}\big\|_{\F}}{\big\|H(P^{(i)})\big\|_{\F}}=0,

namely, increasingly
H(P^{(i)})P^{(i)}\approx P^{(i)}\Lambda_{i}=P^{(i)}\big([P^{(i)}]^{\T}H(P^{(i)})P^{(i)}\big)
,
which means that
P^{(i)}
becomes a more and more accurate approximate solution
to NEPv (
6.3
), even in the absence of knowing whether the entire sequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
converges or not. The latter does require additional condition to establish in the next theorem.

Theorem 6.5
.

Suppose that
the NEPv Ansatz
holds, and let the sequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
be generated by Algorithm
1
and
P_{*}
be an accumulation
point of the sequence.

(a)

{\cal R}(P_{*})
is an accumulation point of the sequence
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
.

(b)

Suppose that
{\cal R}(P_{*})
is an isolated accumulation point of
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
. If

\lambda_{k}(H(P_{*}Q))-\lambda_{k+1}(H(P_{*}Q))>0\quad\mbox{for any $Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)$},

(6.16)

then
the entire sequence
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
converges to
{\cal R}(P_{*})
.

(c)

Suppose that
P_{*}
is an isolated accumulation point of
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
. If

\lambda_{k}(H(P_{*}))-\lambda_{k+1}(H(P_{*}))>0

(6.17)

and if

f(P_{*})>f(P)
for any
P\neq P_{*}
and
{\cal R}(P)={\cal R}(P_{*})
,
i.e.,

f(P)
has unique maximizer in the orbit
\{P_{*}Q\,:\,Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)\}
,
then
the entire sequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
converges to
P_{*}
.

Proof.

See appendix
D
.
∎

6.3
Acceleration by LOCG and Convergence

At Line 3 of Algorithm
1
, an
n\times n
SEP is involved and that can be expensive for large/huge
n
.
As in subsection
3.3
, the same idea for acceleration can be applied here to
speed things up. It has in fact been partially demonstrated in
[
66
, section 5]
on
MBSub.

A variant of LOCG for Acceleration

We adopt the same setup at the beginning of subsection
3.3
up to (
3.12
).
Differently, here we will need a symmetric matrix-valued function
\widetilde{H}(Z)
for the dimensionally reduced
maximization problem (
3.11
) so that
the NEPv Ansatz
can be passed on from
f
with
H
to

\widetilde{f}
with
\widetilde{H}
.

As we commented in Remark
6.1
(i),
the NEPv Ansatz
does not impose any explicit relation between
\mathscr{H}(P)
and
H(P)P
, e.g., through condition (
6.6
),
but in order to figure out a symmetric matrix-valued function
\widetilde{H}(Z)
from
H(P)
, let us assume (
6.6
)
for the moment. Once we figure out what
\widetilde{H}(Z)
should be for the case, we will then show the newly derived
\widetilde{H}(Z)
works for
\widetilde{f}
even without
(
6.6
) as a prerequisite, i.e.,
the NEPv Ansatz
gets passed on from
f
with
H
to

\widetilde{f}
with
\widetilde{H}
.
It follows from (
6.6
) that

W^{\T}\left(H(P)P-\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}\right)=W^{\T}P\,\mathscr{M}(P)\quad\mbox{for $P=WZ$, $Z\in{\rm St}(k,m)$}.

or, equivalently,

\big(W^{\T}H(WZ)W\big)Z-\frac{\partial\widetilde{f}(Z)}{\partial Z}=Z\,\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Z)\quad\mbox{for $Z\in{\rm St}(k,m)$},

(6.18)

where
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Z):=\mathscr{M}(WZ)
. Immediately, (
6.18
) sheds light on
what
\widetilde{H}(Z)
should be and, accordingly, the associated NEPv
for the reduced maximization problem (
3.11
), namely,

\widetilde{H}(Z)Z:=\big(W^{\T}H(WZ)W\big)Z=Z\widetilde{\Omega},\,\,Z\in{\rm St}(k,m).

(6.19)

By Theorem
6.1
, we conclude that, in the case of (
6.6
),

Z\in{\rm St}(k,m)
is a solution to the KKT condition (
3.12
) for
the reduced problem (
3.11b
) if and only if
it is a solution to NEPv (
6.19
)
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}(Z)
is symmetric.

Now that we have found the form of
\widetilde{H}
for
\widetilde{f}
, given
H
for
f
, we can safely relinquish (
6.6
)
and (
6.18
) going forward. In the next lemma, we show that

\widetilde{f}
with
\widetilde{H}
of the reduced problem inherits
the NEPv Ansatz
for
f
with
H
of the original problem.

Lemma 6.1
.

Suppose that
the NEPv Ansatz
holds for
f(P)
with
H(P)
, and let
\mathbb{Z}:=W^{\T}\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,m)
. If

W\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{P}
, then
the NEPv Ansatz
holds for
\widetilde{f}(Z)
defined in (
3.11b
)
with
\widetilde{H}(Z)=W^{\T}H(WZ)W
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{Z}\in{\rm St}(k,m)
and
Z\in\mathbb{Z}:=W^{\T}\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,m)
such that

\tr(\widehat{Z}^{\T}\widetilde{H}(Z)\widehat{Z})\geq\tr(Z^{\T}\widetilde{H}(Z)Z)+\eta.

(6.20)

Set
P=WZ\in\mathbb{P}
because of
W\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\mathbb{P}
and
\widehat{P}=W\widehat{Z}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
. We get
(
6.7
) from (
6.20
). By
the NEPv Ansatz
for
f
with
H
,
there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such that

\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q=W(\widehat{Z}Q)=:W\widetilde{Z}\in\mathbb{P}
and

f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\omega\eta
, i.e.,

\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{Z})=\widetilde{f}(\widehat{Z}Q)=f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\omega\eta=f(WZ)+\omega\eta=\widetilde{f}(Z)+\omega\eta.

Note also
\widetilde{Z}=W^{\T}\widetilde{P}\in W^{\T}\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{Z}
. Hence
the NEPv Ansatz
holds for
\widetilde{f}
with
\widetilde{H}
.
∎

As a consequence of this lemma, and the results in subsections
6.1
and
6.2
, Algorithm
1

is applicable to compute
Z_{\opt}
of (
3.11b
) via NEPv (
6.19
).
We outline the resulting
method in Algorithm
2
, which is an inner-outer iterative scheme for (
1.1
),
where at Line 4
any other method, if known, can be inserted to replace Algorithm
1
to
solve (
3.11b
). The same comments we made in Remark
3.2
and after are applicable to
Algorithm
2
, too.

Algorithm 2

NEPvLOCG: NEPv (
6.3
) solved by LOCG

0:

Symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)
satisfying
the NEPv Ansatz
,
P^{(0)}\in\mathbb{P}
;

0:

an approximate maximizer of (
1.1
).

1:

P^{(-1)}=[\,]
; % null matrix

2:

for

i=0,1,\ldots
until convergence
do

3:

compute
W\in{\rm St}(m,n)
such that
{\cal R}(W)={\cal R}([P^{(i)},\mathscr{R}(P^{(i)}),P^{(i-1)}])

and
P^{(i)}
occupies
the first
k
columns of
W
;

4:

solve (
3.11b
) via NEPv (
6.19
) for
Z_{\opt}
by Algorithm
1

with initially
Z^{(0)}
being the first
k
columns of
I_{m}
;

5:

P^{(i+1)}=WZ_{\opt}
;

6:

end

for

7:

return
the last
P^{(i)}
.

Convergence Analysis

We can perform a convergence analysis of Algorithm
2
similarly to what we did in subsection
3.3
, considering
an ideal situation that
at its Line 4,
Z_{\opt}
is computed to be an exact maximizer of (
3.11
) for simplicity.
We state the convergence result in the next theorem, but omit its proof because of similarity to that
of Theorem
3.4
.

Theorem 6.6
.

Suppose that
the NEPv Ansatz
holds, and let sequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
be generated by Algorithm
2

in which, it is assumed that

Z_{\opt}
is an exact maximizer of (
3.11
).
The following statements hold.

(a)

The sequence
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is monotonically increasing and convergent.

(b)

Any accumulation point
P_{*}
of the sequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is a KKT point of (
1.1
) and
satisfies the necessary conditions in Theorem
6.3
for a global maximizer, i.e.,
(
6.3
) holds for
P=P_{*}
and the eigenvalues of
\Omega=\Omega_{*}:=P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*}

consist of the first
k
largest eigenvalues of
H(P_{*})
.

7
Atomic Functions for NEPv

Armed with the general theoretical framework for the NEPv approach in
section
6
,
in this section, we introduce the notion of atomic functions for NEPv, which serves as
a singleton unit of function on
{\rm St}_{\delta}(k,n)

for which the NEPv approach is guaranteed to work for solving (
1.1
), and more importantly,
the NEPv approach works on any convex composition of atomic functions,
provided that some of the partial derivatives
of the composing convex function are nonnegative.

In what follows, we first formulate two conditions that define
atomic function and prove why the NEPv approach will work on the atomic functions, and then we give concrete examples of
atomic functions that encompass nearly all practical ones that are in use today, and we leave
investigating how the NEPv approach will work on convex compositions of these atomic functions to section
8
.

Combining the results in this section and the next section will yield
a large collection of objective functions, including those in Table
4
, for which

the NEPv Ansatz
holds.

7.1
Conditions on Atomic Functions

Suppose that, for function
f
defined on some neighborhood
{\rm St}_{\delta}(k,n)
of the Stiefel manifold
{\rm St}(k,n)
,
we have already constructed an associated symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.
We are interested in those
that satisfy

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)

\displaystyle=\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}\,f(P)\quad\mbox{for $P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)$},

(7.1a)

and given
P\in\mathbb{P}
and
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such
that
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
and

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}\,f(\widetilde{P})+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}\,f(P),

(7.1b)

where
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}>0,\,\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}\geq 0
, and
\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}
are constants.
Here a subset
\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)
is also involved.

Definition 7.1
.

A function
f
defined on some neighborhood
{\rm St}_{\delta}(k,n)
of
{\rm St}(k,n)
is an
atomic function

for NEPv
if there are
a symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
and
constants
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}>0,\,\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}\geq 0
, and
\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}
such that both conditions in
(
7.1
) hold.

An atomic function according to Definition
7.1
is of the second type
in this paper and is for the NEPv approach, in contrast to the first type that is defined in subsection
4.1
of
Part I for the NPDo approach. As in subsection
4.1
, here it also can be verified that
for two atomic functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
with
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, respectively,
that share the same
\mathbb{P}
, the same constants
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha},\,\underaccent{\bar}{\beta},\,\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}
, and the same
Q
for (
7.1b
),
any linear combination
f:=c_{1}f_{1}+c_{2}f_{2}
with
c_{1}H_{1}+c_{2}H_{2}
for
c_{1},\,c_{2}>0

satisfies (
7.1
), and hence is an atomic function for NEPv as well.

Remark 7.1
.

An alternative to (
7.1b
) is

\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})\leq\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}\,f(\widetilde{P})+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}\,f(P)\quad\mbox{for $P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)$},

(
7.1b
′
)

without referring to an intermediate
\widehat{P}
.
We claim that (
7.1b
′
) is stronger than (
7.1b
),
assuming for any
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such
that
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
. Here is why. Suppose (
7.1b
′
) holds.
Given any
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, let
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
for some
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
. Then
by (
7.1b
′
) we have for any
P\in\mathbb{P}

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})=\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})\leq\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}\,f(\widetilde{P})+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}\,f(P),

yielding (
7.1b
).
In view of this observation, in our later developments, we may verify
(
7.1b
′
) directly if it can be verified.

In relating
\mathscr{H}(P)
to
H(P)
via, e.g., (
6.6
) when (
6.1
) does not hold,

H(P)
in general is not unique
[
46
]
. As a result,
satisfying (
7.1
) may depend on both
f
and
the choice of
H(P)
. In other words, it is possible that the conditions in (
7.1
) are satisfied
for one choice of
H(P)
but may not for another.

Remark 7.2
.

Previously, (
4.1a
) appears explicitly as a partial differential equation (PDE), but (
7.1a
) here
does not.

Nonetheless, it is likely a PDE in disguise, especially when
H(P)P
is related to

\mathscr{H}(P):={\partial f(P)}/{\partial P}

through condition (
6.6
).

The next theorem basically says that the conditions in (
4.1
) that define the atomic function for NPDo are stronger
than the ones in (
7.1
) for NEPv with the generic
H(P)
given by (
6.5
).

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
H(P)
be as in (
6.5
).

(a)

Equation (
4.1a
) implies (
7.1a
) with
\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}=2\gamma
.

(b)

Inequality (
4.1b
)
implies (
7.1b
) with

\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2\alpha
,
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=2\beta
, and
\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=2\gamma
.

Proof.

Assuming (
4.1a
), for
H(P)
as given in (
6.5
), we have
for
P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)

\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)=2\tr\left(P^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\right)=2\gamma\,f(P),

as was to be shown. Assume (
4.1b
). Let

\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n),\,P\in\mathbb{P}
and

let
W_{1}\in{\rm St}(k,k)
be an orthogonal polar factor of

\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)
and
\check{P}=\widehat{P}W_{1}
.

Then we again have (
6.12
) and

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\tr\left(\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P}\right\|_{\tr}\qquad(\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{lm:maxtrace}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\right\|_{\tr}\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\right\|_{\tr}\qquad(\mbox{since $\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}\leq 1$})

\displaystyle=2\tr\left(\check{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\right)\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:NPDo>=NEPv:pf-1}}).

(7.2)

Now use (
4.1b
)

to conclude that there is
W_{2}\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such that
\widetilde{P}=\check{P}W_{2}=\widehat{P}(W_{1}W_{2})\in\mathbb{P}

and

2\tr\left(\check{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)\right)\leq 2\alpha\,f(\widetilde{P})+2\beta f(P).

(7.3)

Combine (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) to get
(
7.1b
) with

\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2\alpha
,
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=2\beta
, and
\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=2\gamma
.
∎

Theorem 7.2
.

Given function
f
defined on
{\rm St}_{\delta}(k,n)
and its associated symmetric
H(P)
that satisfy (
7.1
),
suppose
f(P)\geq 0
for
P\in\mathbb{P}
. Let
g(P)=c[f(P)]^{s}
where
c>0,\,s>1
,
and let its associated symmetric matrix-valued function be
H_{g}(P)=cs\,[f(P)]^{s-1}H(P)
. Then

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H_{g}(P)P)

\displaystyle=s\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}\,g(P)\quad\mbox{for $P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)$},

(7.4a)

and given
P\in\mathbb{P}
and
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such
that
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
and

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{g}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}g(\widetilde{P})+(s\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}-\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha})g(P),

(7.4b)

where
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}
,
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}
are as in (
7.1b
).

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H_{g}(P)P)

\displaystyle=cs\,[f(P)]^{s-1}\,\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)

\displaystyle=cs\,[f(P)]^{s-1}\,\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}\,f(P)\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:cond4AF-NEPv-a}})

\displaystyle=s\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}\,g(P),

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{g}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=cs\,[f(P)]^{s-1}\,\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq cs\,[f(P)]^{s-1}\,\Big[\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}\,f(\widetilde{P})+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}\,[f(P)\Big]\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:cond4AF-NEPv-b}})

\displaystyle=cs\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}\,f(\widetilde{P})\,[f(P)]^{s-1}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}sc\,[f(P)]^{s}

\displaystyle\leq cs\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}\,\left\{\frac{1}{s}[f(\widetilde{P})]^{s}+\frac{s-1}{s}[f(P)]^{s}\right\}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}sg(P)\qquad(\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{lm:YoungIneq-ext}})

\displaystyle=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}g(\widetilde{P})+\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}(s-1)g(P)+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}sg(P)

\displaystyle=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}g(\widetilde{P})+[\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}(s-1)+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}s]g(P),

as expected.
∎

Finally, we show that
the NEPv Ansatz
holds for atomic functions for NEPv. As a corollary, the NEPv approach as laid out in section
6

works on any atomic function for NEPv.

Theorem 7.3
.

The NEPv Ansatz
holds with
\omega=1/\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}
for atomic function
f
with a symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

satisfying the conditions in (
7.1
).

Proof.

Given
P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)
and
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, suppose that (
6.7
) holds, i.e.,

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})\geq\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)+\eta
.
We have by (
7.1
)

\eta+\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}f(P)=\eta+\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)\leq\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})\leq\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}f(\widetilde{P})+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}f(P)

yielding
\eta/\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+f(P)\leq f(\widetilde{P})
, as was to be shown.
∎

7.2
Concrete Atomic Functions

We will show that

[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}
,

[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s}

for integer
m\geq 1
,
s\geq 1
, and also
A\succeq 0
in the case of
m\geq 2
or
s>1
,

(7.5)

with proper symmetric
H(P)
to be given in the theorems and corollaries below,
satisfy (
7.1
)
and hence are atomic functions for NEPv.
Therefore, by Theorem
7.3
,
the NEPv Ansatz
holds for them.
These atomic functions are the same in form as the ones in subsection
4.2

but there are differences as detailed in Table
5
.
When inequality (
4.1b
) or (
7.1b
)
become an equality, there is an important implication when it comes to
verify the corresponding ansatz for
the composition
of atomic functions by a convex function
\phi
, namely, for equality (
4.1b
) or (
7.1b
),
the corresponding partial derivative
\phi_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\partial\phi(\boldsymbol{x})/\partial x_{j}

can be of any sign but in general is required to be nonnegative otherwise. We have seen this
in Theorem
5.1
and will see it again in Theorem
8.1
later.

Table 5:
Concrete atomic functions for NPDo and NEPv

[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}

[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s}

NPDo

m=s=1

m\geq 2
or
s>1

A\succeq 0
,

(
4.1b
) an equality,

(
4.1b
) an inequality,

(
4.1b
) an inequality,

\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
,

\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
.

\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
.

or
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
.

NEPv

m=s=1

m\geq 2
or
s>1

(
7.1b
) an inequality,

(
7.1b
) an equality,

(
7.1b
) an inequality,

\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
.

\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
.

A\succeq 0
,
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
.

* Integer
m\geq 1
, scalar
s\geq 1
and
A
is symmetric.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, integer
m\geq 1
and
f(P)=\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})
for which
we use

H(P)=m\,\Big[D(P^{\T}D)^{m-1}P^{\T}+P(D^{\T}P)^{m-1}D^{\T}\Big],

(7.6)

and thus
H(P)P-\mathscr{H}(P)\equiv P\big[m(D^{\T}P)^{m}\big]
for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)

\displaystyle=2m\,\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})\quad\mbox{for $P\in{\rm St}(k,n)$};

(7.7a)

\displaystyle\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\tr((\widetilde{P}^{\T}D)^{m})+2(m-1)\,\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})\quad\mbox{for $P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{P}$},

(7.7b)

where
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
. They, as argued in Remark
7.1
, imply that (
7.1
) holds with
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=2(m-1)
,
and thus
f(P)=\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})
is an atomic function for NEPv.
Furthermore, any solution
P
to NEPv (
6.3
) with

H(P)
in (
7.6
) such that
(P^{\T}D)^{m}
is symmetric is a solution to the KKT condition (
2.3
) and vice versa.

Proof.

H(P)
in the theorem is in fact the generic one in (
6.5
) for the case
and in the notation of Theorem
6.1
,

\mathscr{M}(P)=m(P^{\T}D)^{m}
. Hence any solution
P
to NEPv (
6.3
) such that
(P^{\T}D)^{m}
is symmetric is a solution to
the KKT condition (
2.3
) and vice versa.

Equation (
7.7a
) can be straightforwardly verified.
Now we prove (
7.7b
).
Inequality (
7.7b
) for
m=1

in fact holds for all
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
. To see this, for
m=1
and
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
,
since
\widetilde{P}^{\T}D\succeq 0
,

\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})=2\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widetilde{P})\leq 2\|\widetilde{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widetilde{P}\|_{\tr}\leq 2\|\widetilde{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}=2\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}D)

by Lemma
B.9
.
In general for
m>1
, suppose both
P^{\T}D\succeq 0
and
\widetilde{P}^{\T}D\succeq 0
.
Then

\displaystyle\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle=2m\,\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}D(P^{\T}D)^{m-1}P^{\T}\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2m\left\|\widetilde{P}^{\T}D(P^{\T}D)^{m-1}P^{\T}\widetilde{P}\right\|_{\tr}\qquad(\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{lm:maxtrace}})

\displaystyle\leq 2m\left\|\widetilde{P}^{\T}D(P^{\T}D)^{m-1}\right\|_{\tr}\|P^{\T}\widetilde{P}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2m\left\|\widetilde{P}^{\T}D(P^{\T}D)^{m-1}\right\|_{\tr}\qquad(\mbox{since $\|P^{\T}\widetilde{P}\|_{2}\leq 1$})

\displaystyle\leq 2\tr((\widetilde{P}^{\T}D)^{m})+2(m-1)\,\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m}),

where the last inequality is due to Lemma
B.5
.

Finally (
7.7
) implies (
7.1
), as
argued in Remark
7.1
.
∎

For any
s>1
,
[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}
is well-defined for any
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}

such that
\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})\geq 0
.
In particular,
[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}
is well-defined
for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
.
Combining Theorems
7.2
and
7.4
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 7.1
.

Let
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, integer
m\geq 1
,
s>1
,
g(P)=[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}
, and
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
.
Let
H(P)

be as in (
7.6
)
and
H_{g}(P)=s\,[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s-1}H(P)

for which
H_{g}(P)P-\partial g(P)/\partial P\equiv P\big[sm\,[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s-1}\,(D^{\T}P)^{m}\big]
.
Then

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H_{g}(P)P)

\displaystyle=2sm\,[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}\quad\mbox{for $P\in\mathbb{P}$},

(7.8a)

\displaystyle\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H_{g}(P)\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2[\tr((\widetilde{P}^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}+2(sm-1)[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}\quad\mbox{for $P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{P}$}.

(7.8b)

They, as argued in Remark
7.1
, imply that (
7.1
) holds with
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=2(sm-1)
,
and thus
g(P)=[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}
for
s>1
is an atomic function for NEPv.

Next we consider
\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})
and its power.

Theorem 7.5
.

Let symmetric
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, integer
m\geq 1
, and

f(P)=\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})
for which we use

H(P):=2m\,A(PP^{\T}A)^{m-1}

(7.9)

and thus
\mathscr{H}(P)\equiv H(P)P
for
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

(a)

For
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, we have

\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)=2m\,f(P)\equiv 2m\,\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m}).

(7.10a)

(b)

Let
P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

(i)

For
m=1
, we always have

\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})=2\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}A\widetilde{P});

(7.10b)

(ii)

For
m>1
, if
A\succeq 0
, then

\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})\leq 2\tr((\widetilde{P}^{\T}A\widetilde{P})^{m})+2(m-1)\,\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m}).

(7.10c)

They, as argued in Remark
7.1
, imply that (
7.1
) holds with
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=2(m-1)
, and
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
,
and thus
f(P)=\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})
is an atomic function for NEPv.

Proof.

With
H(P)
as in (
7.9
), equation (
7.10a
) is straightforwardly
verified.

For
m=1
,
H(P)=2A
and hence immediately we have (
7.10b
).

Consider
m>1
and suppose
A\succeq 0
.
Let
X=A^{1/2}\widetilde{P}
and
Y=A^{1/2}P
, where
A^{1/2}
is the positive semidefinite square root of
A
.
We have

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P}

\displaystyle=2m\,\widetilde{P}^{\T}AP(P^{\T}AP)^{m-2}P^{\T}A\widetilde{P}

\displaystyle=2m\,X^{\T}Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{m-2}Y^{\T}X

\displaystyle=2m\,X^{\T}(YY^{\T})^{m-1}X,

\displaystyle\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H(P)\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle=2m\,\tr(X^{\T}(YY^{\T})^{m-1}X)

\displaystyle=2m\,\tr(XX^{\T}(YY^{\T})^{m-1})

\displaystyle\leq 2\tr((XX^{\T})^{m})+2(m-1)\,\tr((YY^{\T})^{m})\qquad(\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{lm:vN-tr-ineq-ext}})

\displaystyle=2\tr((X^{\T}X)^{m})+2(m-1)\,\tr((Y^{\T}Y)^{m})

\displaystyle=2\tr((\widetilde{P}^{\T}A\widetilde{P})^{m})+2(m-1)\,\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m}),

which is (
7.10c
).
∎

We emphasize that (
7.10a
), (
7.10b
),
and (
7.10c
) actually holds for any
P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
broader than
what the conditions in (
7.1
) entail.
With Theorem
7.5
and using a similar proof to that of
Theorem
7.2
, we get the following corollary that is valid for
all
P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, broader than
simply combining Theorems
7.2
with
7.5
.

Corollary 7.2
.

Let symmetric
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be positive semidefinite, integer
m\geq 1
,
s>1
,
g(P)=[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s}
,
and let
H_{g}(P)=s\,[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s-1}H(P)

for which
\partial g(P)/\partial P\equiv H_{g}(P)P
for
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,

where
H(P)
is as in (
7.9
).
For
P,\,\widetilde{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
we have

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H_{g}(P)P)

\displaystyle=2sm\,[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s},

(7.11a)

\displaystyle\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H_{g}(P)\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2[\tr((\widetilde{P}^{\T}A\widetilde{P})^{m})]^{s}+2(sm-1)[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s}.

(7.11b)

They, as argued in Remark
7.1
, imply that (
7.1
) holds with
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=2(sm-1)
, and
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
,
and thus
g(P)=[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s}
for
s>1
is an atomic function for NEPv.

8
Convex Composition

We are interested in solving the same optimization problem on the Stiefel manifold
{\rm St}(k,n)
as in (
5.1
)
by Algorithm
1
and its accelerating variation in Algorithm
2

with convergence guarantee.
In that regard, we stick to the initial setup at the beginning of section
5
up to the paragraph containing
(
5.3
). We then go along a different path – the path of the NEPv approach. To that end, we will
have to specify what
H(P)
, a symmetric matrix-valued function, to use for a given objective
function
f=\phi\circ T
in (
5.1
),

assuming that a symmetric matrix-valued function
has already been constructed for each component of
T(P)
.

In its generality, each component
f_{i}(P_{i})
of
T(P)
in (
5.3
) may involve a few but not necessarily
all columns of
P
.
It turns out that, for the case when not all
P_{i}=P
, we do not have a feasible way to construct
a symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)
for
f(P)=\phi\circ T(P)
out of those for the components of
T(P)
.
That leaves us the only option of using the generic
H(P)
in (
6.5
):

H(P):=[\mathscr{H}(P)]P^{\T}+P[\mathscr{H}(P)]^{\T}\equiv\left[\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}\right]P^{\T}+P\left[\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}\right]^{\T},

completely ignoring the symmetric matrix-valued functions for the components of
T(P)
already known. Furthermore, with this generic

H(P)
, in order to fulfill
the NEPv Ansatz
, we will have to assume the components of

T(P)
are atomic functions for NPDo satisfying (
5.5
),
which means that
the NPDo Ansatz
will hold for
f
. Consequently, our previous NPDo approach in Part I will work
on such function
f
in the first place, making it unnecessary resort to the NEPv approach for solving
the optimization problem (
5.1
). More detail will be explained in subsection
8.2
.
Besides explaining the extra complexity that not all
P_{i}=P
may bring, in subsection
8.2
we will also demonstrate that
the NEPv approach can still be made to work with the generic
H(P)
, just that the approach may not be as effective as the NPDo approach in Part I.

8.1
All
P_{i}
are the entire
P

Our focus is on
the case when all
P_{i}=P
, i.e., each component
f_{i}(P_{i})=f_{i}(P)
.
Specifically, we will consider a special case of
T(P)
in (
5.3
):

T_{0}(P)=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}(P)\\
f_{2}(P)\\
\vdots\\
f_{N}(P)\\
\end{bmatrix},

(8.1)

where
f_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq N
are atomic functions for NEPv, whose associated
symmetric matrix-valued functions are
H_{i}(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for
1\leq i\leq N
, respectively.

Our first task is to create a proper symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

to go with
f=\phi\circ T_{0}
from
H_{i}(P)
for
1\leq i\leq N
. To that end,
we will follow what we did in subsection
6.3
to first figure out what

H(P)
should be for the circumstance

when (
6.6
) holds for each
f_{i}
, namely,

H_{i}(P)P-\frac{\partial f_{i}(P)}{\partial P}=P\,\mathscr{M}_{i}(P)\quad\mbox{for $1\leq i\leq N$},

(8.2)

and then show that the newly created
H(P)
can serve the purpose for us as far as inheriting

the NEPv Ansatz
from
f_{i}
with
H_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq N
is concerned,
without the need to assume (
8.2
) anymore.

Recall notation
\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})
in (
5.2
) for the
i
th partial derivative of
\phi(\boldsymbol{x})
.
For
f=\phi\circ T_{0}
, we have

\mathscr{H}(P):=\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T_{0}(P))\,\frac{\partial f_{i}(P)}{\partial P},

and hence naturally, we may choose

H(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T_{0}(P))\,H_{i}(P),

(8.3)

for which, with (
8.2
), we find

\displaystyle H(P)P-\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T_{0}(P))\,\left(H_{i}(P)P-\frac{\partial f_{i}(P)}{\partial P}\right)

\displaystyle=P\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T_{0}(P))\,\mathscr{M}_{i}(P)

\displaystyle=:P\mathscr{M}(P).

(8.4)

Therefore the symmetric
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
in (
8.3
) fits the one suggested by
Theorem
6.1
. In particular, any solution
P_{*}
to NEPv (
6.3
)
with
H(P)
given by (
8.3
) satisfies the KKT condition (
2.3
) if

\mathscr{M}(P_{*})
defined in (
8.4
) is symmetric and vice versa, as guaranteed by Theorem
6.1
.

Next we will show that
f=\phi\circ T_{0}
with
H(P)
in (
8.3
) inherits

the NEPv Ansatz
from
f_{i}
with
H_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq N

without assuming (
8.2
).

To that end, we place some consistency conditions upon all components of
T_{0}(P)
in (
8.1
) as follows:
for
1\leq i\leq N

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H_{i}(P)P)

\displaystyle=\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}_{i}\,f_{i}(P)\quad\mbox{for $P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)$},

(8.5a)

and given
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
and
P\in\mathbb{P}
, there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such
that
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
and

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}f_{i}(\widetilde{P})+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}f_{i}(P),

(8.5b)

where
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}>0,\,\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}\geq 0
, and

\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}_{i}=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}
are constants.

It is important to keep in mind that some of the inequalities in (
8.5b
) may actually be equalities,
e.g., for
f_{i}(P)=\tr(P^{\T}A_{i}P)
it is an equality by Theorem
4.3
.

On the surface, it looks like that each
f_{i}
is simply an atomic function for NEPv, but there are three
built-in consistency requirements in (
8.5
) among
all
f_{i}
: 1) the same
\mathbb{P}
for all; 2) the same
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}
for all, and
3) the same
Q
to give
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q
for all.

Theorem 8.1
.

Consider
f=\phi\circ T_{0}
, where
T_{0}(\cdot)
takes the form in (
8.1
) and
\phi
is convex and differentiable with partial derivatives denoted by

\phi_{i}
as in (
5.2
). Let
H(P)
be given by (
8.3
) with
H_{i}(P)
for
1\leq i\leq N

satisfying (
8.5
).
If
\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\geq 0
for those
i
for which (
8.5b
) does not become an equality, then

the NEPv Ansatz
with
\omega=1/\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}
holds for
f=\phi\circ T_{0}
with
H
.

Proof.

Given
P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)
and
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, suppose that (
6.7
) holds, i.e.,

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})\geq\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)+\eta
.
Write

\boldsymbol{x}=T_{0}(P)\equiv[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}]^{\T},\quad\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}=T_{0}(\widetilde{P})\equiv[\widetilde{x}_{1},\widetilde{x}_{2},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{N}]^{\T},

i.e.,
x_{i}=f_{i}(P)
and
\widetilde{x}_{i}=f_{i}(\widetilde{P})
.
Noticing
H(P)
in (
8.3
), we have by (
8.5
)

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(P^{\T}H_{i}(P)P)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}_{i}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,x_{i},\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:cond4AF-NEPv:cvx-a}})

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,(\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}\widetilde{x}_{i}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}x_{i}),

where the last inequality is due to
\phi_{i}\geq 0
when the corresponding (
8.5b
)
does not become an equality.
Plug them into
\eta+\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)\leq\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})
and simplify the resulting inequality with
the help of
\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}_{i}=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}
to get

\eta/\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\T}\boldsymbol{x}=\eta/\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,x_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\widetilde{x}_{i}=\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\T}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}.

Finally apply Lemma
B.3
to yield
f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\eta/\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}
.
∎

With Theorem
8.1
come the general results established in section
6
.
In particular, Algorithm
1
(NEPvSCF) and its accelerating variation in Algorithm
2
can be applied to find a maximizer of (
5.1
),
except that the calculation of
Q_{i}
at Line 4 of Algorithm
1
remains to be specified. This missing detail is in general
dependent of the particularity of the mapping
T_{0}
and the convex function
\phi
.
What we will do in Examples
8.1
and
8.3
below provides some ideas on this matter.

In the rest of this section,

A_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
are at least symmetric
and
D_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
for
1\leq i\leq t
.

Example 8.1
.

Consider
T_{1a}
in (
5.13
), as a special case of
T_{0}
, and optimization problem (
5.1
) with

f=\phi\circ T_{1a}
. For this example, we will use

H(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(T_{1a}(P))\underbrace{2A_{i}}_{=:H_{i}(P)}+\sum_{j=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+j}(T_{1a}(P))\,\underbrace{\big(D_{j}P^{\T}+PD_{j}^{\T}\big)}_{=:H_{\ell+j}(P)},

(8.6)

for which
H(P)P-\mathscr{H}(P)\equiv P\big[\mathscr{D}(P)^{\T}P\big]
for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, where, as in (
5.14
),

\mathscr{D}(P)=\sum_{j=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+j}(T_{1a}(P))\,D_{j}.

Any solution
P
to NEPv (
6.3
) with

H(P)
in (
8.6
) such that
[\mathscr{D}(P)]^{\T}P
is symmetric is a solution to the KKT condition (
2.3
)
and vice versa.

It can be seen that (
8.5b
) is an equality for
1\leq i\leq\ell
and hence
it does not need to require
\phi_{i}\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
. In addition to this, instead of treating each

H_{\ell+j}(P)
separately, we can treat
H_{\ell+j}(P)
for
1\leq j\leq t
collectively all at once
through
\mathscr{D}(P)
, as
we did in (
5.2
), making all
\phi_{\ell+j}\geq 0
unnecessary as well. A much more improved
version of Theorem
8.1

is stated as Theorem
8.2
below,

according to which, the best
Q_{i}
at Line 4 of Algorithm
1

when applied to
\phi\circ T_{1a}

is an orthogonal polar factor of
[\widehat{P}^{(i)}]^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P^{(i)})
.
A special case of
T_{1a}
is:
t=0
,
k=1
,
P_{i}=\boldsymbol{p}
(a unit vector) for
1\leq i\leq\ell
, which gives the main problem of
[
5
]

(in the paper,
\phi(\boldsymbol{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\psi_{i}(x_{i})

for
\boldsymbol{x}=[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}]^{\T}
with each
\psi_{i}
being a convex function of a single-variable).

Theorem 8.2
.

Consider
f=\phi\circ T_{1a}
, and let
\mathscr{D}(P)
be as in (
5.14
)
and
H(P)
as in (
8.6
).
Given
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n),\,P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, let
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q
where

Q
is an orthogonal polar factor of
\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)
.
If

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})\geq\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)+\eta,

then

f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\frac{1}{2}\eta+\delta,

where
\delta=\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)\|_{\tr}-\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P})
.
In particular,
the NEPv Ansatz
holds with
\omega=1/2
.

Proof.

Along the lines of the proof of Theorem
8.1
, here we will have

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,x_{i}+2\sum_{i=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,x_{\ell+i},

\displaystyle\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)\|_{\tr}

\displaystyle=\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P))\qquad(\mbox{since $\widetilde{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)=Q^{\T}[\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)]\succeq 0$})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\widetilde{x}_{\ell+i},

(8.7)

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}A_{i}\widehat{P})+2\sum_{i=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}D_{i}P^{\T}\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}A_{i}\widetilde{P})+2\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\widetilde{x}_{i}+2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)\|_{\tr}-2\delta

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\widetilde{x}_{i}+2\sum_{i=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\widetilde{x}_{\ell+i}-2\delta,

where the last equality is due to (
8.7
).
Plug them into
\eta+\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)\leq\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})
and simplify the resulting inequality to get

\frac{1}{2}\eta+\delta+\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\T}\boldsymbol{x}\leq\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\T}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}},

and then apply Lemma
B.3
to conclude the proof.
∎

Theorem
8.2
improves Theorem
8.1
in that
the objective value
increases additional
\delta
more. We notice, by Lemma
B.9
, that

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P})\leq\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{\tr}\leq\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)\|_{\tr}\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}\leq\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)\|_{\tr}

and hence

\delta\geq 0
and it is strict when any one of the inequalities above is strict.
Theorem
8.2
compares favorably against Theorem
5.2
. Both are about
mapping
T_{1a}
, but Theorem
8.2
puts no condition on symmetric matrices
A_{i}
and
no condition on the partial derivatives, whereas Theorem
5.2
requires all
A_{i}\succeq 0
and

\phi_{i}\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
.

Also note that, in Theorem
8.2
,
\widetilde{P}
satisfies

\widetilde{P}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P)\succeq 0
. Along the same line of the proof of Theorem
6.3
,
we establish another necessary condition in Corollary
8.1
for any maximizer
P_{*}
of (
5.1
)
with
T=T_{1a}
, besides the ones in
Theorem
6.3
.

Corollary 8.1
.

Consider (
5.1
) with
T=T_{1a}
and let
H(P)
be as in (
8.6
).
If
P_{*}
is a maximizer of (
5.1
), then we
have not only NEPv (
6.3
) satisfied by
P=P_{*}
and
\Omega=\Omega_{*}:=P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*}
whose eigenvalues
consist of the
k
largest ones of
H(P_{*})
, but also
P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{D}(P_{*})\succeq 0
.

Example 8.2
.

Consider
T_{2a}
, a special case of
T_{2}
in (
5.10
),

T_{2a}\,:\,P\in{\rm St}(k,n)\to T_{2a}(P):=\begin{bmatrix}\|P^{\T}A_{1}P\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\|P^{\T}A_{\ell}P\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\|P^{\T}D_{1}\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\|P^{\T}D_{t}\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell+t}.

(8.8)

Either
\ell=0
or
t=0
is allowed. Notice that

\|P^{\T}A_{i}P\|_{\F}^{2}=\tr((P^{\T}A_{i}P)^{2}),\quad\|P^{\T}D_{j}\|_{\F}^{2}=\tr(P^{\T}D_{j}D_{j}^{\T}P).

For optimization problem (
5.1
) with

f=\phi\circ T_{2a}
, we will use

H(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(T_{2a}(P))\,\underbrace{4A_{i}PP^{\T}A_{i}}_{=:H_{i}(P)}+\sum_{j=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+j}(T_{2a}(P))\,\underbrace{D_{j}D_{j}^{\T}}_{=:H_{\ell+j}(P)},

(8.9)

for which
\mathscr{H}(P)\equiv H(P)P
for
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

If all
A_{i}\succeq 0
, then, by Theorem
7.5
,
the consistency conditions in (
8.5
) are satisfied with
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)
,
Q=I_{k}
,

\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2
,
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}=2
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{\ell+j}=0
for
1\leq j\leq t
.
Note, for the example, (
8.5b
) for
\ell+1\leq i\leq\ell+t
are equalities and hence
Theorem
8.1
requires

\phi_{i}\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
only.
Optimization problem (
5.1
) with
T=T_{2a}
for
t=0

and

\phi(\boldsymbol{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{i}

gives the key optimization problem in the uniform multidimensional scaling (UMDS) method
[
79
]
.

Comparing the conclusion here and that of Example
5.2
(for all
P_{i}=P
), we don’t require
\phi_{\ell+j}\geq 0
for
1\leq j\leq t
here, everything else being equal. In particular, both require
A_{i}\succeq 0
for all
i
. Next, we explain how to make the NEPv approach work on
f=\phi\circ T_{2a}
even if some
A_{i}\not\succeq 0
, yielding yet another example for which the NEPv
approach works but the NPDo approach may not.
Let
\delta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\widehat{A}_{i}=A_{i}-\delta_{i}I\succeq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
.
This is always possible by letting
\delta_{i}
be some lower bound of the eigenvalues of
A_{i}
, and numerically,
\delta_{i}
can be estimated cheaply
[
81
]
. Notice that

\tr((P^{\T}A_{i}P)^{2})=\tr((P^{\T}\widehat{A}_{i}P)^{2})+2\delta_{i}\tr(P^{\T}\widehat{A}_{i}P)+k\delta_{i}^{2}.

Define

\widehat{T}_{2a}\,:\,P\in{\rm St}(k,n)\to\widehat{T}_{2a}(P):=\begin{bmatrix}\tr((P^{\T}\widehat{A}_{1}P)^{2})\\
\vdots\\
\tr((P^{\T}\widehat{A}_{\ell}P)^{2})\\
\tr(P^{\T}\widehat{A}_{1}P)\\
\vdots\\
\tr(P^{\T}\widehat{A}_{\ell}P)\\
\|P^{\T}D_{1}\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\|P^{\T}D_{t}\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\ell+t},

(8.10)

and an affine transformation:
\mathscr{A}\,:\,\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\ell+t}\,\to\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell+t}
by

\boldsymbol{x}=\mathscr{A}(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}})=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{(1:\ell)}+2\Delta\,\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{(\ell+1:2\ell)})+k\boldsymbol{d}\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{(2\ell+1:2\ell+t)})\end{bmatrix},

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{(i:j)}
is the sub-vector of
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}
from its
i
th entry to
j
th entry,
\Delta=\diag(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{\ell})\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times\ell}
,
and
\boldsymbol{d}^{\T}=[\delta_{1}^{2},\ldots,\delta_{\ell}^{2}]
. Finally,
f(P)=\hat{\phi}\circ\widehat{T}_{2a}(P)
where

\hat{\phi}(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}})=\phi(\boldsymbol{x})\equiv\phi(\mathscr{A}(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}))
is convex in
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}

[
13
, p.79]
. It can be verified that

\hat{\phi}_{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}):=\frac{\partial\hat{\phi}(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}})}{\partial\hat{x}_{i}}=\begin{cases}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x}),&\quad\mbox{for $1\leq i\leq\ell$},\\
2\delta_{i-\ell}\phi_{i-\ell}(\boldsymbol{x}),&\quad\mbox{for $\ell+1\leq i\leq 2\ell$},\\
\phi_{i-\ell}(\boldsymbol{x}),&\quad\mbox{for $2\ell+1\leq i\leq 2\ell+t$}.\end{cases}

Theorem
8.1
is now applicable to
f(P)=\hat{\phi}\circ\widehat{T}_{2a}(P)
, but requiring only

\phi_{i}\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
and without requiring any of
A_{i}\succeq 0
.

Example 8.3
.

Consider
T_{3a}
, a special case of
T_{3}
in (
5.11
),

T_{3a}\,:\,P\in{\rm St}(k,n)\to T_{3a}(P):=\begin{bmatrix}\tr((P^{\T}A_{1}P)^{m_{1}})\\
\vdots\\
\tr((P^{\T}A_{\ell}P)^{m_{\ell}})\\
\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m_{\ell+1}})\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell+1},

(8.11)

where integer
m_{i}\geq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq\ell+1
and
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. It reduces to an even more special case of Example
8.1

if all
m_{i}=1
for
1\leq i\leq\ell+1
.
Either
\ell=0
or without the last component
\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m_{\ell+1}})
is allowed.
For optimization problem (
5.1
) with

f=\phi\circ T_{3a}
, we will use

H(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(T_{3a}(P))\,\underbrace{2m_{i}\,A_{i}(PP^{\T}A_{i})^{m_{i}-1}}_{=:H_{i}(P)}\\
+\phi_{\ell+1}(T_{3a}(P))\,\underbrace{m_{\ell+1}\,\big[D(P^{\T}D)^{m_{\ell+1}-1}P^{\T}+P(D^{\T}P)^{m_{\ell+1}-1}D^{\T}\big]}_{=:H_{\ell+1}(P)},

(8.12)

for which
H(P)P-\mathscr{H}(P)\equiv P\big[\phi_{\ell+1}(T_{3a}(P))\,m_{\ell+1}\,(D^{\T}P)^{m_{\ell+1}}\big]
for
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
.
Any solution
P
to NEPv (
6.3
) with

H(P)
in (
8.12
) such that
(D^{\T}P)^{m_{\ell+1}}
is symmetric is a solution to the KKT condition (
2.3
).

Suppose all
A_{i}\succeq 0
and let
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
.
Then
the consistency conditions in (
8.5
) are satisfied with

\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2
,
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}=2(m_{i}-1)
for
1\leq i\leq\ell+1
, by
Theorems
7.4
and
7.5
.
In applying Theorem
8.1
with
T_{0}=T_{3a}
we need
\phi_{i}\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell+1

because now we are not sure if any of the inequalities in (
8.5b
) is an equality.

Lastly, the best
Q_{i}
at Line 4 of Algorithm
1
when applied to

\phi\circ T_{3a}

is an orthogonal polar factor of
[\widehat{P}^{(i)}]^{\T}D
and vice versa.

8.2
Not all
P_{i}
are the entire
P

We now consider
T(P)
in (
5.3
) in its generality, i.e., some of the
P_{i}

do not contain all columns of
P
.
To proceed, first we attempt to construct a symmetric
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

from
H_{i}(P_{i})
for each component
f_{i}(P_{i})
of
T(P)
.
Assume, for the moment, that

H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}-\frac{\partial f_{i}(P_{i})}{\partial P_{i}}=P_{i}\mathscr{M}_{i}(P_{i}),

(8.13)

where
\mathscr{M}_{i}(P_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{i}\times k_{i}}
.
Recalling the expression for
\mathscr{H}(P)
in (
5.4a
) as a linear combination of

\frac{\partial f_{i}(P_{i})}{\partial P_{i}}J_{i}^{\T}
,
we have, by using (
8.13
) and
P_{i}=PJ_{i}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f_{i}(P_{i})}{\partial P_{i}}J_{i}^{\T}

\displaystyle=\Big[H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}-P_{i}\mathscr{M}_{i}(P_{i})\Big]J_{i}^{\T}

\displaystyle=H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}J_{i}^{\T}-PJ_{i}\mathscr{M}_{i}(P_{i})J_{i}^{\T}

\displaystyle=H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}J_{i}^{\T}P^{\T}P-PJ_{i}\mathscr{M}_{i}(P_{i})J_{i}^{\T}

\displaystyle=\Big[H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}J_{i}^{\T}P^{\T}+PJ_{i}P_{i}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})\Big]P-P\Big[J_{i}P_{i}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})P+J_{i}\mathscr{M}_{i}(P_{i})J_{i}^{\T}\Big],

and as a result

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(P):=\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T(P))\frac{\partial f_{i}(P_{i})}{\partial P_{i}}J_{i}^{\T}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T(P))\Big[H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}J_{i}^{\T}P^{\T}+PJ_{i}P_{i}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})\Big]\right)}_{=:H(P)}P

\displaystyle\quad-P\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T(P))\Big[J_{i}P_{i}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})P+J_{i}\mathscr{M}_{i}(P_{i})J_{i}^{\T}\Big]\right)}_{=:\mathscr{M}(P)}\,,

taking the form

H(P)P-\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}=P\mathscr{M}(P),

(8.14)

where the symmetric
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is given by

H(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T(P))\Big[H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}P_{i}^{\T}+P_{i}P_{i}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})\Big].

(8.15)

This construction reminds us of the technique that was first used in
[
77
]
for OCCA to turn the KKT condition
into an NEPv, where a single term
D
is converted to
DP^{\T}+PD^{\T}
. The same technique was later used in

[
74
,
67
,
66
]
and in this paper too in (
6.5
).

Although
H(P)
in (
8.15
) seems to be a good candidate to build a framework with
as laid out in section
6
, there is
an apparent obstacle that is hard, if at all possible, to cross over. Recall the key foundation
in the consistency conditions in (
8.5
). For the current case, we would need similar ones, i.e.,
conditions such as

\displaystyle\tr(P_{i}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i})

\displaystyle=\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}_{i}\,f_{i}(P_{i})\quad\mbox{for $P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)$},

(8.16a)

and given
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
and
P\in\mathbb{P}
, there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such
that
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q\in\mathbb{P}
and

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}_{i}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})\widehat{P}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}f_{i}(\widetilde{P}_{i})+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}f_{i}(P_{i}),

(8.16b)

where
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}>0,\,\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}\geq 0
, and

\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}_{i}=\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}
are constants.
In return, we would then relate
\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)
to

\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}x_{i}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})

by equality and bound

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})
from above in terms of

\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}x_{i}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})

and

\sum_{i}\gamma_{i}\widetilde{x}_{i}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})

where
\boldsymbol{x}\equiv[x_{i}]=T(P)
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\equiv[\widetilde{x}_{i}]=T(\widetilde{P})
. The former is rather straightforward because

\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)=2\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T(P))\tr(P_{i}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i})=2\sum_{i=1}^{N}\gamma_{i}x_{i}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x}),

but the latter seems to be insurmountable because

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})=2\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}P_{i}^{\T}\widehat{P})

and it is not clear how to bound
\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}P_{i}^{\T}\widehat{P})
from above,
given (
8.16b
).
So the route via
H(P)
in (
8.15
) is blocked. This ends our first attempt of constructing
H(P)
from

H_{i}(P_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq N
.

We will seek an alternative route, as our second attempt. Assume the following setting: among the
N
components

f_{i}(P_{i})
,
the first
N_{1}
of them involve
P_{i}
that are not the entire
P
but
the last
N_{2}
do, i.e.,

P_{i}=P\quad\mbox{for $N_{1}+1\leq i\leq N$},

(8.17)

where
N_{1}+N_{2}=N
.
We resort the generic symmetric matrix-valued function in (
6.5
)
for the first
N_{1}
components
f_{i}(P_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq N_{1}
but the individual
H_{i}(P)

for the last
N_{2}
components
f_{i}(P)
for
N_{1}+1\leq i\leq N
to get:

H(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(T(P))\left(\frac{\partial f_{i}(P_{i})}{\partial P_{i}}P_{i}^{\T}+P_{i}\left[\frac{\partial f_{i}(P_{i})}{\partial P_{i}}\right]^{\T}\right)+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(T(P))\,H_{i}(P).

(8.18)

In doing so, we completely ignore
H_{i}(P_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq N_{1}
that we presumably already know and should take
advantage of but cannot.
To proceed, we will also need
the same consistency conditions as in (
5.5
) for NPDo for
f_{i}(P_{i})
for

1\leq i\leq N_{1}
but the ones in (
8.16
) for
f_{i}(P)
for

N_{1}+1\leq i\leq N
. This makes
the first
N_{1}
components of
T(P)
atomic functions for both NPDo and for NEPv.

In this setting
N_{1}=0
or
N_{2}=0
are allowed. The case
N_{1}=0
is the one we have already dealt with
in subsection
8.1
, and for the case
N_{2}=0
, the NPDo approach can
be applied in the first place.
It remains to conquer the case both
N_{1},\,N_{2}\geq 1
. Our next theorem will help us to do that.

Theorem 8.3
.

Consider
f=\phi\circ T
, where
T(\cdot)
takes the form in (
5.3
)
with (
8.17
) and
\phi

is convex and differentiable with partial derivatives denoted by

\phi_{i}
as in (
5.2
). Let
H(P)
be given by (
8.18
).
Suppose that

(i)

the conditions in (
5.5
) hold for
1\leq i\leq N_{1}
,

(ii)

the conditions in (
8.16
) hold with
Q=I_{k}
for
N_{1}+1\leq i\leq N
,

(iii)

\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\geq 0
for those
1\leq i\leq N_{1}

for which (
5.5b
) does not become an equality
and for those
N_{1}+1\leq i\leq N

for which (
8.16b
) does not become an equality,

(iv)

\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2\alpha
for
\alpha
in (
5.5b
)
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}
in (
8.16b
).

Then
the NEPv Ansatz
holds with
\omega=1/(2\alpha)
and with the
Q
-matrix
specified in the proof.

Proof.

Given
\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
and
P\in\mathbb{P}\subseteq{\rm St}(k,n)
,
suppose that (
6.7
) holds, i.e.,

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})\geq\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)+\eta
.
Write

\boldsymbol{x}=T(P)\equiv[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{N}]^{\T},\quad\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}=T(\widetilde{P})\equiv[\widetilde{x}_{1},\widetilde{x}_{2},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{N}]^{\T},

where
x_{i}=f_{i}(P_{i})
and
\widetilde{x}_{i}=f_{i}(\widetilde{P}_{i})
, and write

\mathscr{H}_{i}(P_{i})=\frac{\partial f_{i}(P_{i})}{\partial P_{i}}
.
For
H(P)
in (
8.18
), we have by (
5.5
)
and (
8.16
)

\displaystyle\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(P_{i}^{\T}\mathscr{H}_{i}(P_{i}))+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(P^{\T}H_{i}(P)P)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\gamma_{i}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,x_{i}+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}_{i}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,x_{i},\qquad\mbox{(by \eqref{eq:cond4AF-NPDo:cvx-a} and \eqref{eq:tr-diff-eq:cvx:Hi(Pi)})}.

Let
W_{1}\in{\rm St}(k,k)
be an orthogonal polar factor of
\widehat{P}^{\T}\underaccent{\bar}{\scrH}(P)
and
Z=\widehat{P}W_{1}
,
where

\underaccent{\bar}{\scrH}(P):=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(T(P))\frac{\partial f_{i}(P_{i})}{\partial P_{i}}J_{i}^{\T}.

We have

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}_{i}(P_{i})P_{i}^{\T}\widehat{P})+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}\mathscr{H}_{i}(P_{i})J_{i}^{\T}P^{\T}\widehat{P})+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}\underaccent{\bar}{\scrH}(P)P^{\T}\widehat{P})+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\underaccent{\bar}{\scrH}(P)\|_{\tr}\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{P}^{\T}\underaccent{\bar}{\scrH}(P)\|_{\tr}+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=2\tr(Z^{\T}\underaccent{\bar}{\scrH}(P))+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(Z^{\T}H_{i}(P)Z)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(Z_{i}^{\T}\mathscr{H}_{i}(P_{i}))+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(Z^{\T}H_{i}(P)Z),

where
Z_{i}=ZJ_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq N
are submatrices of
Z
.
Now use the second consistency condition in (
5.5b
) to get
W_{2}\in{\rm St}(k,k)
and set

\widetilde{P}=ZW_{2}=\widehat{P}(W_{1}W_{2})=:\widehat{P}Q
. Then

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\tr(Z_{i}^{\T}\mathscr{H}_{i}(P_{i}))+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}H_{i}(P)\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,(\alpha\widetilde{x}_{i}+\beta_{i}x_{i})+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,(\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}\widetilde{x}_{i}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,(\alpha\widetilde{x}_{i}+\beta_{i}x_{i})+\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,(2\alpha\widetilde{x}_{i}+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}x_{i}).

Plug them into
\eta+\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)\leq\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})
and simplify the resulting inequality with
the help of
\gamma_{i}=\alpha+\beta_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq N_{1}
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\gamma}_{i}=2\alpha+\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}

for
N_{1}+1\leq i\leq N
to get

\eta/(2\alpha)+\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\T}\boldsymbol{x}=\eta/(2\alpha)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,x_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}(\boldsymbol{x})\,\widetilde{x}_{i}=\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\T}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}.

Finally apply Lemma
B.3
to yield
f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\eta/(2\alpha)
.
∎

We now explain why the resulting Algorithm
1
is not going to be competitive to
Algorithm
1
per SCF iterative step in the case
N_{2}=0
. First
both algorithms work due to the same set of consistency conditions in (
5.5
). Next
carefully examining the proof of Theorem
8.3
, we find that

Q_{i}
at Line 4 of Algorithm
1
is the product of two
k\times k
orthogonal matrices,

W_{1}
from an orthogonal polar factor of
[\widehat{P}^{(i)}]^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})
and
W_{2}
from the second consistency condition
in (
5.5b
). Algorithm
1
also needs
Q_{i}
at its line 4 but just one
orthogonal matrix dictated by the second consistency condition
in (
5.5
),
making its SCF step cheaper, not to mention
there is an additional partial eigendecomposition to compute
at Line 3 of Algorithm
1
. Having said that, the significance of Theorem
8.3

lies in the mixture case: both
N_{1}\geq 1
and
N_{2}\geq 1
, for which the theorem ensures that
Algorithm
1
can still be applied with guaranteed convergence. The next example falls into such a category.

Example 8.4
.

Consider
T_{4}
that shares some similarity with
T_{2}
in (
5.10
) and
T_{2a}
in (
8.8
):

T_{4}\,:\,P\in{\rm St}(k,n)\to T_{4}(P):=\begin{bmatrix}\|P_{1}^{\T}A_{1}P_{1}\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\|P_{\ell}^{\T}A_{\ell}P_{\ell}\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\tr(P^{\T}B_{1}P)\\
\vdots\\
\tr(P^{\T}B_{t}P)\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell+t},

(8.19)

where
A_{i}\succeq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
and
B_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for
1\leq i\leq t
are symmetric.
Each
\|P_{i}^{\T}A_{i}P_{i}\|_{\F}^{2}=\tr\big(\big[P_{i}^{\T}A_{i}P_{i}\big]^{2}\big)
is an atomic function of NPDo by Theorem
4.4
,
and each
\tr(P^{\T}B_{i}P)
is an atomic function of NEPv (by Theorem
7.5
)
but may not be an atomic function of NPDo unless
B_{i}\succeq 0
also.
We can be do away with
B_{i}\not\succeq 0
by shifting
B_{i}
to
B_{i}-\delta I\succeq 0
as we did in
the second part of Example
8.2
, but the shift works only if the corresponding
partial derivative
\phi_{\ell+i}\geq 0
.
If we do not have that, then the NPDo approach is not guaranteed to work even with the shifting technique.
With Theorem
8.3
, however,
we can make the NEPv approach work with

\displaystyle H(P)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(T(P))\left(\frac{\partial\tr\big(\big[P_{i}^{\T}A_{i}P_{i}\big]^{2}\big)}{\partial P_{i}}P_{i}^{\T}+P_{i}\left[\frac{\partial\tr\big(\big[P_{i}^{\T}A_{i}P_{i}\big]^{2}\big)}{\partial P_{i}}\right]^{\T}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+i}(T(P))\,2B_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\phi_{i}(T(P))\,4\Big(A_{i}P_{i}P_{i}^{\T}AP_{i}P_{i}^{\T}+P_{i}P_{i}^{\T}A_{i}P_{i}P_{i}^{\T}A_{i}\Big)+\sum_{i=1}^{t}\phi_{\ell+i}(T(P))\,2B_{i},

assuming
\phi_{i}\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq\ell
but no requirement to impose on
\phi_{\ell+i}
for
1\leq i\leq t
is necessary,
because all
\tr\big(\big[P_{i}^{\T}A_{i}P_{i}\big]^{2}\big)
share the same

\alpha=1
and
\beta=3
in (
5.5b
)
while all
\tr(P^{\T}B_{i}P)
share the same
\underaccent{\bar}{\alpha}=2
and
\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}=0
in (
8.16b
)
which is also an equality for the case.

Remark 8.1
.

We conclude section
8
by commenting on the applicability of the results of this section
to the objective functions
in Table
1
via convex compositions of atomic functions for NEPv.
Essentially our results are applicable to
all but OLDA and SumTR, for which the corresponding composing functions
\phi
are

x_{2}/x_{1}
and
x_{2}/x_{1}+x_{3}
, respectively. Both are non-convex, and yet
the NEPv Ansatz
still holds for
OLDA but does not for SumTR.
With the generic
H(P)
as in (
6.5
), SumCT
can be handled too through the convex composition of atomic functions but the resulting NEPv approach may not be competitive to the NPDo approach,
as we have argued in subsection
8.2
.
The composing function for OCCA is
x_{2}/\sqrt{x_{1}}
, which is not convex but whose square
x_{2}^{2}/x_{1}
is convex for
x_{2}\geq 0
and
x_{1}>0
.
For
\Theta
TR, the theory in subsection
8.1
can only handle
0\leq\theta\leq 1/2

and also on the objective function squared, however:

[f(P)]^{2}=\phi\circ T(P)\quad\mbox{with}\,\,T(P)=\begin{bmatrix}\tr(P^{\T}BP)\\
\tr(P^{\T}AP)\\
\tr(P^{\T}D)\end{bmatrix},\,\,\phi(\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{(x_{2}+x_{3})^{2}}{x_{1}^{2\theta}},

(8.20)

where
\boldsymbol{x}\equiv[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]^{\T}
.
This
T
has the form of
T_{1a}
of Example
8.1
and it can be verified that the associated symmetric matrix-valued
function
H(P)
by (
8.6
) differs from the one in (
6.4
)
[
67
]
by a scalar factor only.
We claim that
\phi
is convex for
x_{1}>0
and
x_{2}+x_{3}\geq 0
, provided
0\leq\theta\leq 1/2
,
and, since also
\phi_{3}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\partial\phi(\boldsymbol{x})/\partial x_{3}\geq 0
for
x_{1}>0
and
x_{2}+x_{3}\geq 0
, Theorem
8.2
applies.

We note that

\phi_{0}(x,y)={y^{2}}/{x^{2\theta}}

for

x>0
and
y\geq 0

is convex if
0\leq\theta\leq 1/2
but is not convex if
\theta>1/2
. In fact, the Hessian matrix of
\phi_{0}
is

\begin{bmatrix}2\theta(2\theta+1){y^{2}}/{x^{2\theta+2}}&-4\theta y/{x^{2\theta+1}}\\
-4\theta y/{x^{2\theta+1}}&2/{x^{2\theta}}\end{bmatrix}=\frac{2}{x^{2\theta}}\begin{bmatrix}y/x&\\
&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\theta(2\theta+1)&-2\theta\\
-2\theta&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}y/x&\\
&1\end{bmatrix}

which is positive semidefinite for
x>0
and
y\geq 0
if and only if
0\leq\theta\leq 1/2
.
This implies

\phi(\boldsymbol{x})\equiv\phi_{0}(x_{1},x_{2}+x_{3})
is convex for
x_{1}>0
and
x_{2}+x_{3}\geq 0
, provided
0\leq\theta\leq 1/2
.
However, the results in

[
67
]
says that the NEPv approach works on
f
for
\Theta
TR for
0\leq\theta\leq 1
,
much bigger range for
\theta

than what Theorem
8.2
for
f^{2}
implies.
Theorem
8.2
also yields an inequality on how much the objective function squared,

f^{2}
, increases, in contrast to the previous (
6.11
) which is for the original objective function,
f
, for
0\leq\theta\leq 1
and OCCA.

In any case, the best
Q_{i}
at Line 4 of Algorithm
1
when applied to
\Theta
TR
is an orthogonal polar factor of
[\widehat{P}^{(i)}]^{\T}D
and
\mathbb{P}={\rm St}(k,n)_{D+}
.

9
A Brief Comparison of the NPDo and NEPv Approaches

The developments of the frameworks for both NPDo and NEPv follow the same pattern: an ansatz that implies
the global convergence of their respective SCF iterations, the definition of atomic functions for an approach, and the fulfillment of
the ansatz by the atomic functions for the approach and their convex compositions. Subtly, between the two approaches, there are differences in applicabilities
and numerical implementations, making them somewhat complementary to each other.

Table 6:
NPDo
vs.
NEPv on three convex compositions of matrix traces

f

NPDo

NEPv

conditions

by

conditions

by

\phi\circ T_{1a}

A_{i}\succeq 0,\,1\leq i\leq\ell
,

Thm.
5.2

none

Thm.
8.2

\phi_{j}\geq 0,\,1\leq j\leq\ell

\phi\circ T_{2a}

A_{i}\succeq 0,\,1\leq i\leq\ell
,

Expl.
5.2

\phi_{j}\geq 0,\,1\leq j\leq\ell

Expl.
8.2

\phi_{j}\geq 0,\,1\leq j\leq\ell+t

\phi\circ T_{3a}

A_{i}\succeq 0,\,1\leq i\leq\ell
,

Expl.
5.3

A_{i}\succeq 0,\,1\leq i\leq\ell
,

Expl.
8.3

\phi_{j}\geq 0,\,1\leq j\leq\ell+1

,
