*
\phi_{j}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\partial\phi(\boldsymbol{x})/\partial x_{j}
for
\boldsymbol{x}=[x_{j}]
.

We outline some notable differences below.

The NEPv approach requires weaker conditions.

We have
demonstrated that
the NDPo Ansatz
demands more on an objective function than
the NEPv Ansatz
, and hence
the NEPv approach provably works on a wider collection of maximization problems (
1.1
) on the Stiefel manifold
than the NPDo approach.

(i)

The NPDo approach requires that the KKT
condition
\mathscr{H}(P)\equiv{\partial f(P)}/{\partial P}=P\Lambda

is a polar decomposition at optimality, in order for the approach to be even considered,
whereas the NEPv approach does not impose that
the KKT condition must be an NPDo at optimality;

(ii)

The NPDo Ansatz
implies
the NEPv Ansatz
with the generic symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)
in (
6.5
) (see Theorem
6.2
);

(iii)

Atomic functions for NPDo are also atomic functions for NEPv with the generic symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)
in (
6.5
) (see Theorem
7.1
);

(iv)

Among those in Table
1
, the NEPv approach is guaranteed to work for three more
than the NPDo approach does (Table
2

vs.
Table
4
);

(v)

When it comes to the concrete atomic functions
[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s}

where integer
m\geq 1
and scalar
s\geq 1
,
it is required that
A\succeq 0
always for the NPDo approach, whereas for the NEPv approach
A
being symmetric
suffices for
m\in\{1,2\}
and
s=1
(see Examples
8.1
and
8.2
);

(vi)

As a further demonstration, in Table
6
, we summarize
what are required by both approaches on four convex compositions of matrix-trace functions, and
it clearly indicates that NEPv requires weaker conditions than NPDo does for

\phi\circ T_{1a}
,
\phi\circ T_{2a}
, and
\phi\circ T_{4}
.

The NPDo approach is easier to use.

The NPDo approach, if it provably works, is easier to implement and more flexible
to use.

(a)

The NPDo approach relies on
SVDs of tall and skinny matrices
[
17
,
24
]
during its SCF
iterations, whereas the NEPv approach needs solutions to potentially large scale eigenvalue problems
[
3
,
36
,
41
,
51
,
56
]
, not to mention that the NEPv approach requires constructing a symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)
.

(b)

In general for the NPDo approach,
the atomic functions in a convex composition are allowed to be of submatrices of
P

consisting of a few, not necessarily all, columns of
P
, such as
P_{i}
in

\tr(P_{i}^{\T}A_{i}P_{i})
of SumCT in Table
1
, but, more generally,
different
P_{i}
can share common columns of
P
, whereas no
P_{i}
in SumCT share common columns of
P
;
For the NEPv approach, allowing such flexibility in the involved atomic functions in the convex composition
forces us to use the generic symmetric matrix-valued function
H(P)
in (
6.5
)
and ask for the same conditions as the NPDo approach requires, and hence we may as well go for
the NPDo approach in the first place,
as we have argued in subsection
8.2
.

10
Concluding Remarks

The first order optimality condition, also known as the KKT condition, for optimizing a function
f
over the Stiefel manifold takes the form

\mathscr{H}(P):=\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}=P\Lambda\quad\mbox{with}\quad\Lambda^{\T}=\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},\quad P^{\T}P=I_{k}.

(10.1)

This is an
n\times k
matrix equation in
P
on the Stiefel manifold, upon noticing
\Lambda=[P^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P)+\mathscr{H}(P)^{\T}P]/2
.
Any maximizer is a solution. Except for very special objective functions such as
\tr(P^{\T}AP)
or
\tr(P^{\T}D)
, solving this equation rightly
and efficiently for a maximizer is a challenging task. For example, it often has infinitely many solutions and maximizers
hide among them. Hence we need to not only solve the nonlinear equation of the KKT condition but also find the right ones.
Inspired by recent works
[
67
,
66
,
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
]
, in this paper,
we establish two unifying frameworks, one for the NEPv approach and the other for the NPDo approach, for optimization on the Stiefel manifold.
Our frameworks are built upon two fundamental ansatzes,

the NPDo Ansatz
and
the NEPv Ansatz
. When a respective ansatz is
satisfied, the corresponding approach, the NPDo or NEPv approach, is guaranteed to work
in the sense of global convergence from any given initial point.
To expand the applicability of the approaches, we propose the theories of atomic functions for each approach
and show that any convex composition
\phi\circ T
of atomic functions for any of the two approaches satisfies the corresponding ansatz under some mild conditions.
It is demonstrated that the
commonly used
matrix-trace functions

[\tr((P^{\T}AP)^{m})]^{s},\quad[\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})]^{s}\quad\mbox{for integer $m\geq 1$ and real scalar $s\geq 1$}

are concrete atomic functions for both approaches (
A
may be required to be positive semidefinite depending on circumstances),
and that nearly all optimization problems on the Stiefel manifold recently investigated in the literature
for various machine learning applications are about some compositions of these concrete matrix-trace functions. Although not all of them are convex compositions, some may still satisfy
the NEPv Ansatz
.
These concrete atomic functions in combination with convex compositions lead to a large collection of objective functions on which
one of or both approaches work.

However when an ansatz fails to hold for a given objective function, the conclusions from
our global convergence analysis will likely fail. In the case for
the NEPv approach, a remedy via the level-shifting SCF exists to ensure locally linear convergence
when
f(P)
is right-unitarily
invariant
[
4
]
or when
f(P)
contains and increases with
\tr(P^{\T}D)

[
46
]
,
where sharp estimations of linear convergence rate are obtained.
But there are more works to do, especially for
the NPDo approach for which a remedy remains to be found. In
[
62
]
, it is investigated how

\tr(P^{\T}D)
may help determine a particular
P
among all orthonormal basis matrices of a subspace.

Not all objective functions (or their simple transformations like
f^{2}
for
\Theta
TR as explained in Remark
8.1
) that satisfy
the ansatz(es) take the form of some convex compositions of atomic functions, even for some of those in Table
1
.
For example, in the case of OLDA,
f(P)=\phi\circ T(P)
where
\phi(\boldsymbol{x})=x_{1}/x_{2}
and

T(P)=\begin{bmatrix}\tr(P^{\T}AP)\\
\tr(P^{\T}BP)\end{bmatrix}
.
Although this
f(P)
is not a convex composition of
\tr(P^{\T}AP)
and
\tr(P^{\T}BP)
, it still
satisfies
the NEPv Ansatz
. In fact, OLDA is a special case of
\Theta
TR:
\theta=1
and
D=0
, and
inequality (
6.11
) applies and yields, for OLDA,

f(\widetilde{P})\geq f(P)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{s_{k}(B)}{S_{k}(B)}\Big[\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})-\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)\Big],

assuming
B\succeq 0
and
\rank(B)>n-k
,
where
\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}
and
H(P)
is given by (
6.4
)
upon setting
\theta=1
and
D=0
.

Numerical demonstrations on the performances of the NEPv approach and the NPDo approach on various machine learning applications
have been well documented by the author and his collaborators in
their recent works
[
67
,
66
,
74
,
77
]
.
We expect more to come as the unifying frameworks in this paper have significantly expanded the domains on which the approaches provably work.

Throughout the article, we limit ourselves to the Stiefel manifold in the standard inner product

\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle=\boldsymbol{y}^{\T}\boldsymbol{x}
for
\boldsymbol{x},\,\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and to the field of real numbers, because that is where
most optimization on the Stiefel manifold from machine learning dominantly falls into. But our developments can be extended to
the field of complex numbers and other inner-products, separately and combined.
For the case of the field of complex numbers, minor modifications suffice: replacing
transpose
(\cdot)^{\T}
with conjugate transpose
(\cdot)^{\HH}
and
\tr((P^{\T}D)^{m})
with
\Re(\tr((P^{\HH}D)^{m}))

(where
\Re(\cdot)
takes the real part of a complex number).
However, extending to the case of the
M
-inner product requires additional algebraic manipulations and analysis. An outline on how to proceed is explained
in appendix
E
.

Appendix A
Canonical Angles

We introduce a metric on
Grassmann manifold

\mathscr{G}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the collection of all
k
-dimensional subspaces in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let

{\cal X}={\cal R}(X)
and
{\cal Y}={\cal R}(Y)
be two points in
\mathscr{G}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
X,\,Y\in{\rm St}(k,n)
. The canonical angles

\theta_{1}({\cal X},{\cal Y})\geq\cdots\geq\theta_{k}({\cal X},{\cal Y})
between
{\cal X}
and
{\cal Y}
are defined by
[
58
]

0\leq\theta_{i}({\cal X},{\cal Y}):=\arccos\sigma_{i}(X^{\T}Y)\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\quad\mbox{for $1\leq i\leq k$},

and accordingly,
the diagonal matrix of the canonical angles between
{\cal X}
and
{\cal Y}
is

\Theta({\cal X},{\cal Y})=\diag(\theta_{1}({\cal X},{\cal Y}),\dots,\theta_{k}({\cal X},{\cal Y}))\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}.

It is known that

\displaystyle\dist_{2}({\cal X},{\cal Y})

\displaystyle:=\|\sin\Theta({\cal X},{\cal Y})\|_{2}=\sin\theta_{1}({\cal X},{\cal Y}),

(A.1)

\displaystyle\dist_{\F}({\cal X},{\cal Y})

\displaystyle:=\|\sin\Theta({\cal X},{\cal Y})\|_{\F}=\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sin^{2}\theta_{i}({\cal X},{\cal Y})\Big]^{1/2}

(A.2)

are two unitarily invariant metrics on the
Grassmann manifold

\mathscr{G}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

[
59
, p.99]
.

Appendix B
Preliminary Lemmas

In this section, we collect a few results, some known and some likely new, that we need in our proofs.
We will point out an earliest reference, if known, to each one. Some likely appear before but we are not aware of
any reference to.
For completeness, we will provide proofs for those we cannot find references.

Lemma B.1

(Young’s Inequality)
.

Given
a,\,b\geq 0
, and
p,\,q\geq 1
such that
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, we have

a^{1/p}b^{1/q}\leq\frac{1}{p}\,a+\frac{1}{q}\,b.

In particular for
p=q=2
, it becomes
2\sqrt{ab}\leq a+b
.

Lemma B.2
.

For
a,\,b\geq 0
, and
\mu,\,\nu\geq 0
such that
\mu+\nu\geq 1
, we have

a^{\mu}b^{\nu}\leq\frac{\mu}{\mu+\nu}\,a^{\mu+\nu}+\frac{\nu}{\mu+\nu}\,b^{\mu+\nu}.

Proof.

Let
\tau:=\mu+\nu\geq 1
. Then
\mu/\tau+\nu/\tau=1
. Using Young’s Inequality, we get

a^{\mu}b^{\nu}=\left(a^{\mu/\tau}b^{\nu/\tau}\right)^{\tau}\leq\left(\frac{\mu}{\tau}\,a+\frac{\nu}{\tau}\,b\right)^{\tau}\leq\frac{\mu}{\tau}\,a^{\tau}+\frac{\nu}{\tau}\,b^{\tau},

as was to be shown, where the last inequality follows from that fact that
x^{\tau}
with
\tau\geq 1
is convex on
[0,\infty)
.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\phi\,:\,\mathfrak{D}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be convex and differentiable and let
\boldsymbol{x},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathfrak{D}
,
where
\mathfrak{D}
is a convex domain in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.
If

\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\T}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\geq\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})^{\T}\boldsymbol{x}+\eta,

then
\phi(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}})\geq\phi(\boldsymbol{x})+\eta
.

Proof.

Likely, the result of this lemma is known, and it has a short proof.
Since
\phi
is convex, we have

\displaystyle\phi(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle\geq\phi(\boldsymbol{x})+[\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})]^{\T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}-\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle=[\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})]^{\T}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{x}}+\phi(\boldsymbol{x})-[\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})]^{\T}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\geq[\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})]^{\T}\boldsymbol{x}+\eta+\phi(\boldsymbol{x})-[\nabla\phi(\boldsymbol{x})]^{\T}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\phi(\boldsymbol{x})+\eta,

as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma B.4

(von Neumann’s trace inequality
[
64
]
,
[
28
, p.183]
)
.

For
B,\,C\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, we have

|\tr(B^{\T}C)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(B)\,\sigma_{i}(C).

In the next four lemmas, any arbitrary nonnegative power of a positive semidefinite matrix
B
is understood as

B^{\mu}=U\Lambda^{\mu}U^{\T}
for any
\mu\geq 0
, where
B=U\Lambda U^{\T}
is the eigendecomposition of
B
,
and
\Lambda^{\mu}
is obtained by taking the
\mu
th power of every diagonal entry of
\Lambda
.

Lemma B.5
.

For
B,\,C\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
that are positive semidefinite, and
\mu,\,\nu\geq 0
such that
\mu+\nu\geq 1
, we have

\tr(B^{\mu}C^{\nu})\leq\|B^{\mu}C^{\nu}\|_{\tr}\leq\frac{\mu}{\mu+\nu}\,\tr(B^{\mu+\nu})+\frac{\nu}{\mu+\nu}\,\tr(C^{\mu+\nu}).

Proof.

That
\tr(B^{\mu}C^{\nu})\leq\|B^{\mu}C^{\nu}\|_{\tr}
is a corollary of Weyl’s majorant theorem
[
8
, p.42]
.
Let
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such that
Q^{\T}B^{\mu}C^{\nu}\succeq 0
, which yields

\|B^{\mu}C^{\nu}\|_{\tr}=\tr(Q^{\T}B^{\mu}C^{\nu})
. Note that
B\succeq 0
and thus

(Q^{\T}B^{\mu})^{\T}(Q^{\T}B^{\mu})=B^{2\mu}
, implying the singular values of
Q^{\T}B^{\mu}
are given by
\{[\sigma_{i}(B)]^{\mu}\}_{i=1}^{k}
.
Since
C\succeq 0
, the singular values of
C^{\nu}
are
\{[\sigma_{i}(C)]^{\nu}\}_{i=1}^{k}
. Hence by Lemma
B.4
and then by Lemma
B.2
,
we get

\displaystyle\|B^{\mu}C^{\nu}\|_{\tr}

\displaystyle=\tr([Q^{\T}B^{\mu}]C^{\nu})

(B.1)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}[\sigma_{i}(B)]^{\mu}\,[\sigma_{i}(C)]^{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu+\nu}\,[\sigma_{i}(B)]^{\mu+\nu}+\frac{\nu}{\mu+\nu}\,[\sigma_{i}(C)]^{\mu+\nu}\right)

(B.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{\mu+\nu}\,\tr(B^{\mu+\nu})+\frac{\nu}{\mu+\nu}\,\tr(C^{\mu+\nu}),

as expected.
∎

Lemma B.6
.

For
B,\,C\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
where
C
is positive semidefinite, and
\nu\geq 0
, we have

\tr(BC^{\nu})\leq\|BC^{\nu}\|_{\tr}\leq\frac{1}{1+\nu}\,\tr((Q^{\T}B)^{1+\nu})+\frac{\nu}{1+\nu}\,\tr(C^{1+\nu}),

where
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such that
Q^{\T}B\succeq 0
.

Proof.

Again
\tr(BC^{\nu})\leq\|BC^{\nu}\|_{\tr}
is a corollary of Weyl’s majorant theorem. Despite that
B
may not be positive semidefinite (possibly not even symmetric), we still have (
B.1
) with
\mu=1
so long as
Q^{\T}BC^{\nu}\succeq 0
. Also note

(Q^{\T}B)^{\T}(Q^{\T}B)=B^{\T}B
and hence
the singular values of
Q^{\T}B
are given by
\{\sigma_{i}(B)\}_{i=1}^{k}
. So
we still get (
B.2
) with
\mu=1
.
The proof is completed upon noticing the eigenvalues of
Q^{\T}B
are the same as the singular values of
B
.
∎

Lemma B.7
.

For
X,\,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
\mu,\,\nu\geq 0
, we have

\tr((X^{\T}X)^{\mu}X^{\T}Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{\nu})\\
\leq\frac{1+2\mu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\tr((X^{\T}X)^{\mu+\nu+1})+\frac{1+2\nu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\tr((Y^{\T}Y)^{\mu+\nu+1}).

Proof.

The singular values of
(X^{\T}X)^{\mu}X^{\T}
and
Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{\nu}
are
\{[\sigma_{i}(X)]^{1+2\mu}\}_{i=1}^{k}

and
\{[\sigma_{i}(Y)]^{1+2\nu}\}_{i=1}^{k}
, respectively. Hence by Lemma
B.4
and then by Lemma
B.2
,
we get

\displaystyle\tr((X^{\T}X)^{\mu}X^{\T}Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{\nu})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}[\sigma_{i}(X)]^{1+2\mu}[\sigma_{i}(Y)]^{1+2\nu}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{1+2\mu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}[\sigma_{i}(X)]^{2(\mu+\nu+1)}+\frac{1+2\nu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}[\sigma_{i}(Y)]^{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1+2\mu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\tr((X^{\T}X)^{\mu+\nu+1})+\frac{1+2\nu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\tr((Y^{\T}Y)^{\mu+\nu+1}),

as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma B.8
.

For
X,\,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
\mu,\,\nu\geq 0
, we have

\|(X^{\T}X)^{\mu}X^{\T}Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{\nu}\|_{\F}^{2}\\
\leq\frac{1+2\mu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\|(X^{\T}X)^{\mu+\nu+1}\|_{\F}^{2}+\frac{1+2\nu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\|(Y^{\T}Y)^{\mu+\nu+1}\|_{\F}^{2}.

Proof.

Notice that

\displaystyle\|(X^{\T}X)^{\mu}X^{\T}Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{\nu}\|_{\F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\tr((X^{\T}X)^{\mu}X^{\T}Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{2\nu}Y^{\T}X(X^{\T}X)^{\mu})

\displaystyle=\tr(X(X^{\T}X)^{2\mu}X^{\T}Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{2\nu}Y^{\T}).

Hence, similarly to the proof of Lemma
B.7
, we get

\displaystyle\|(X^{\T}X)^{\mu}X^{\T}Y(Y^{\T}Y)^{\nu}\|_{\F}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}[\sigma_{i}(X)]^{2+4\mu}[\sigma_{i}(Y)]^{2+4\nu}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{2+4\mu}{4(\mu+\nu+1)}[\sigma_{i}(X)]^{4(\mu+\nu+1)}+\frac{2+4\nu}{4(\mu+\nu+1)}[\sigma_{i}(Y)]^{4(\mu+\nu+1)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1+2\mu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\tr((X^{\T}X)^{2(\mu+\nu+1)})+\frac{1+2\nu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\tr((Y^{\T}Y)^{2(\mu+\nu+1)})

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1+2\mu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\|(X^{\T}X)^{\mu+\nu+1}\|_{\F}^{2}+\frac{1+2\nu}{2(\mu+\nu+1)}\,\|(Y^{\T}Y)^{\mu+\nu+1}\|_{\F}^{2},

as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma
B.8
is actually not needed in this article. We present it here because of its similarity to Lemma
B.7
.
A special of it for
\mu=\nu=0
is hidden in the proofs in
[
79
]
.

Lemma B.9

(
[
77
, Lemma 3]
)
.

For
H\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, we have

|\tr(H)|\leq\|H\|_{\tr}.

If

|\tr(H)|=\|H\|_{\tr},

then

H
is symmetric and is either positive semi-definite when
\tr(H)\geq 0
, or negative semi-definite when
\tr(H)\leq 0
.

Remark B.1

(
[
66
]
)
.

As a corollary of Lemma
B.9
, for any
H\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, if
H\not\succeq 0

(which means either
H
is not symmetric or
H
is symmetric but indefinite or even negative semidefinite),

then
\tr(H)<\|H\|_{\tr}
.
Now let
H=U\Sigma V^{\T}
be the SVD of
H

[
24
]
where
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}

and set
Q=UV^{\T}
, an orthogonal polar factor of
H
. Then

Q^{\T}H=V\Sigma V^{\T}\succeq 0
and
\tr(Q^{\T}H)=\|H\|_{\tr}>\tr(H)
.

Lemma B.10
.

Let
H\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be symmetric and
P_{*}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
whose column space
{\cal R}(P_{*})
is the invariant subspace
of
H
associated with its
k
largest eigenvalues. Suppose that
\lambda_{k}(H)-\lambda_{k+1}(H)>0
.
Given
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, let

\eta=\tr(P_{*}^{\T}HP_{*})-\tr(P^{\T}HP),\quad\epsilon=\sqrt{\frac{\eta}{\lambda_{k}(H)-\lambda_{k+1}(H)}}.

Then
3
3

3

The first inequality in (
B.3
) actually holds so long as

{\cal R}(P_{*})
is a
k
-dimensional invariant subspace of
H
. It is the second inequality
that needs the condition of
{\cal R}(P_{*})
being associated with the
k
largest eigenvalues of
H
.

\frac{\|HP-P(P^{\T}HP)\|_{\F}}{\lambda_{1}(H)-\lambda_{n}(H)}\leq\|\sin\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\|_{\F}\leq{\epsilon}.

(B.3)

Proof.

We know that
\eta\geq 0
by Fan’s trace minimization principle
[
20
]
.
The second inequality in (
B.3
) is
[
35
, Theorem 1]
and can also be derived from some of the estimates in

[
68
, Chapter 3]
and by a minor modification to the proof of
[
39
, Theorem 2.2]
. It remains to show
the first inequality in (
B.3
).
Expand
P
to
[P,P_{\bot}]\in{\rm St}(n,n)
. We find

[P,P_{\bot}]^{\T}\big(HP-P(P^{\T}HP)\big)=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
P_{\bot}^{\T}HP\end{bmatrix},

implying

\displaystyle\|HP-P(P^{\T}HP)\|_{\F}

\displaystyle=\left\|[P,P_{\bot}]^{\T}\big(HP-P(P^{\T}HP)\big)\right\|_{\F}

\displaystyle=\|P_{\bot}^{\T}HP\|_{\F}

\displaystyle=\|P_{\bot}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P\|_{\F},

(B.4)

for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
because
P_{\bot}^{\T}P=0
. But in what follows, we will take
\xi=[\lambda_{1}(H)+\lambda_{n}(H)]/2
.
Next we expand
P_{*}
to
[P_{*},P_{*\bot}]\in{\rm St}(n,n)
. Since the column space of
P_{*}
is the invariant subspace
of
H
associated with the
k
largest eigenvalues of
H
, we have

H[P_{*},P_{*\bot}]=[P_{*},P_{*\bot}]\begin{bmatrix}P_{*}^{\T}HP_{*}&\\
&P_{*\bot}^{\T}HP_{*\bot}\end{bmatrix},

and

\displaystyle P_{\bot}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P

\displaystyle=P_{\bot}^{\T}[P_{*},P_{*\bot}]\begin{bmatrix}P_{*}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P_{*}&\\
&P_{*\bot}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P_{*\bot}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}P_{*}^{\T}\\
P_{*\bot}^{\T}\end{bmatrix}P

\displaystyle=P_{\bot}^{\T}P_{*}P_{*}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P_{*}P_{*}^{\T}P+P_{\bot}^{\T}P_{*\bot}P_{*\bot}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P_{*\bot}P_{*\bot}^{\T}P.

(B.5)

Noticing that

\displaystyle\|P_{\bot}^{\T}P_{*}\|_{\F}=\|P_{*\bot}^{\T}P\|_{\F}=\|\sin\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\|_{\F},

\displaystyle\|P_{*}^{\T}P\|_{2}\leq 1,\qquad\|P_{\bot}^{\T}P_{*\bot}\|_{2}\leq 1,

and, for
\xi=[\lambda_{1}(H)+\lambda_{n}(H)]/2
,

\displaystyle\|P_{*}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P_{*}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|H-\xi I\|_{2}=\frac{1}{2}[\lambda_{1}(H)-\lambda_{n}(H)],

\displaystyle\|P_{*\bot}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P_{*\bot}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|H-\xi I\|_{2}=\frac{1}{2}[\lambda_{1}(H)-\lambda_{n}(H)],

we get from (
B.5
),

\displaystyle\|P_{\bot}^{\T}(H-\xi I)P\|_{\F}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|H-\xi I\|_{2}\|\sin\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\|_{\F}

\displaystyle=[\lambda_{1}(H)-\lambda_{n}(H)]\|\sin\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\|_{\F},

which together with (
B.4
) yield the first inequality in (
B.3
), as expected.
∎

The next lemma are likely well-known. For example, they are implied in the discussion in
[
66
]
before

[
66
, Lemma 3.2]
there.

Lemma B.11
.

Let
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

(a)

\tr(P^{\T}B)\leq\|B\|_{\tr}
for any
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
;

(b)

\tr(P^{\T}B)=\|B\|_{\tr}
where
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
if and only if
B=P\Lambda
with
\Lambda\succeq 0
;

(c)

We have

\max_{P\in{\rm St}(k,n)}\tr(P^{\T}B)=\|B\|_{\tr}

and the optimal value
\|B\|_{\tr}
is achieved by any orthogonal polar factor
P_{*}
of
B
.

Lemma
B.11
says that
\tr(P^{\T}B)
is bounded above by
\|B\|_{\tr}
always and the upper bound

\|B\|_{\tr}
is achieved
by any orthogonal polar factor
P_{*}
of
B
and also any maximizer of
\tr(P^{\T}B)
over
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
is an orthogonal polar factor of
B
.
For numerical computation, an orthogonal polar factor of
B
can be constructed from the thin SVD
B=U\Sigma V^{\T}
as

P_{*}=UV^{\T}
of
B
. Conceivably,
the closer
\tr(P^{\T}B)
is to the upper bound, the closer
P
approaches to an orthogonal polar factor of
B
.
The results of the next lemma quantify the last statement.

Lemma B.12
.

Let
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and suppose
\rank(B)=k
. Let
P_{*}
be the unique
orthogonal polar factor of
B
. Given
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, let

\eta=\|B\|_{\tr}-\tr(P^{\T}B),\quad\epsilon=\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\sigma_{\min}(B)}}.

(a)

We have
4
4

4

The proof of the second inequality in (
B.6
) is actually through proving

\|\sin\frac{1}{2}\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\|_{\F}\leq\frac{1}{2}{\epsilon}
, which is stronger.
Since
{\cal R}(P_{*})
is the same as
{\cal R}(B)
here, it can be replaced with
{\cal R}(B)
.

\frac{\|B-P(P^{\T}B)\|_{\F}}{\|B\|_{2}}\leq\|\sin\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\|_{\F}\leq{\epsilon}\,;

(B.6)

(b)

If
P^{\T}B\succ 0
, then

\|P-P_{*}\|_{\F}\leq\left(1+\frac{2\|B\|_{2}}{\sigma_{\min}(B)+\sigma_{\min}(P^{\T}B)}\right){\epsilon}\,;

(B.7)

(c)

If
{\cal R}(P)={\cal R}(P_{*})
, in which case
\sin\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))=0
, then

\|P-P_{*}\|_{\F}\leq{\epsilon}\,.

(B.8)

Proof.

Let
\theta_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
be the canonical angles between
subspaces
{\cal R}(P)
and
{\cal R}(P_{*})
where
\pi/2\geq\theta_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\theta_{k}\geq 0
.
Then the singular values of
P^{\T}P_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
are
\cos\theta_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. Let
B=P_{*}\Lambda
be the polar decomposition of
B
.
We have

P^{\T}B=P^{\T}P_{*}\Lambda
and hence

\|B\|_{\tr}-\eta=\tr(P^{\T}B)=\tr([P^{\T}P_{*}]\Lambda)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(B)\cos\theta_{k-i+1}

(B.9)

by Lemma
B.4
.
Noticing
\|B\|_{\tr}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(B)
, we get from (
B.9
)

\displaystyle\eta\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(B)\big[1-\cos\theta_{k-i+1}\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(B)\big[2\sin^{2}(\theta_{k-i+1}/2)\big]

(B.10)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}(B)\cdot\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}\theta_{k-i+1}

(B.11)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{\min}(B)\|\sin\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\|_{\F}^{2},

yielding the second inequality in (
B.6
), where we have used

\sin\theta\leq 2\sin\frac{\theta}{2}=\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos(\theta/2)}\leq\sqrt{2}\sin\theta\quad\mbox{for}\quad 0\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}.

Now we expand
P
to
[P,P_{\bot}]\in{\rm St}(n,n)
. We find

[P,P_{\bot}]^{\T}\big(B-P(P^{\T}B)\big)=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
P_{\bot}^{\T}B\end{bmatrix},

implying

\|B-P(P^{\T}B)\|_{\F}=\left\|[P,P_{\bot}]^{\T}\big(B-P(P^{\T}B)\big)\right\|_{\F}=\|P_{\bot}^{\T}B\|_{\F}.

(B.12)

It follows from the CS decomposition
[
58
]
that the singular values of
P_{\bot}^{\T}P_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times(n-k)}
comes from

\sin\theta_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, with possibly some additional zeroes.
We have

P_{\bot}^{\T}B=P_{\bot}^{\T}P_{*}\Lambda
and hence

\|P_{\bot}^{\T}B\|_{\F}=\|P_{\bot}^{\T}P_{*}\Lambda\|_{\F}\leq\|P_{\bot}^{\T}P_{*}\|_{\F}\|B\|_{2}=\|B\|_{2}\|\sin\Theta({\cal R}(P),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\|_{\F},

which, together (
B.12
), yield the first inequality in (
B.6
).

Next, we show (
B.7
). The proof technique is borrowed from
[
76
, Lemma 4.1]
and

[
62
, section 3.1]
.
Suppose now that
P^{\T}B\succ 0
. Following the proof of
[
76
, Lemma 4.1]
, we
can conclude that there exists
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
such that
\widetilde{P}=P_{*}Q^{\T}
satisfies

\|P_{*}-\widetilde{P}\|_{\F}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}4\sin^{2}\frac{\theta_{i}}{2}\leq\frac{2\eta}{\sigma_{\min}(B)},

(B.13)

where we have used (
B.11
) for the last inequality.
Adopting the argument in
[
62
, section 3.1]
upon noticing

P^{\T}B=I_{k}\cdot(P^{\T}B)
and
\widetilde{P}^{\T}B=Q\cdot(P_{*}^{\T}B)
are two polar decompositions, we have,
by
[
38
, Theorem 1]
,

\displaystyle\|I_{k}-Q\|_{\F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sigma_{\min}(B)+\sigma_{\min}(P^{\T}B)}\|P^{\T}B-\widetilde{P}^{\T}B\|_{\F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\|B\|_{2}}{\sigma_{\min}(B)+\sigma_{\min}(P^{\T}B)}\|P-\widetilde{P}\|_{\F}\,,

(B.14)

and hence

\|P-P_{*}\|_{\F}\leq\|P-\widetilde{P}\|_{\F}+\|\widetilde{P}-P_{*}\|_{\F}\\
=\|P-\widetilde{P}\|_{\F}+\|I-Q\|_{\F},

which, together with (
B.13
) and (
B.14
), lead to (
B.7
).
We may simply combine the second inequality in (
B.6
) with
[
62
, Theorem 3.1]

to obtain a bound on
\|P-P_{*}\|_{\F}
, but then the resulting bound will be bigger than the right-hand side of (
B.7
) by
a factor of
\sqrt{2}
.

Consider now item (c) for which
{\cal R}(P)={\cal R}(P_{*})
. Then
P=P_{*}W
for some
W\in{\rm St}(k,k)
.
Recall that
B=P_{*}\Lambda
is the polar decomposition of
B
, and hence
\Lambda\succ 0
and its eigenvalues are the singular values of
B
. Let
\Lambda=U\Gamma U^{\T}
be the eigendecomposition of
\Lambda
where
U\in{\rm St}(k,k)
and

\Gamma=\diag(\sigma_{1}(B),\ldots,\sigma_{k}(B))
.
Write
U^{\T}WU=[w_{ij}]\in{\rm St}(k,k)
.
We know
-1\leq w_{ii}\leq 1
for
1\leq i\leq N
.
We still have by (
B.9
)

\eta=\tr(\Lambda)-\tr(W^{\T}\Lambda)=\tr(U\Gamma U^{\T})-\tr(W^{\T}U\Gamma U^{\T})=\tr(\Gamma)-\tr(U^{\T}W^{\T}U\Gamma),

yielding

\displaystyle\eta=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(1-w_{ii})\sigma_{i}(B)\geq\sigma_{\min}(B)\sum_{i=1}^{k}(1-w_{ii}),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}(1-w_{ii})\leq\frac{\eta}{\sigma_{\min}(B)}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}.

(B.15)

We have

\|P-P_{*}\|_{\F}^{2}=\|W-I\|_{\F}^{2}=\|U^{\T}(W-I)U\|_{\F}^{2}=\|U^{\T}WU-I\|_{\F}^{2}
, and thus

\displaystyle\|P-P_{*}\|_{\F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(w_{ii}-1)^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j\neq i}|w_{ij}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(w_{ii}-1)^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(1-w_{ii}^{2})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{k}(1-w_{ii})\leq\epsilon^{2},\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:polar2max:pf-8}})

as was to be shown.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of Theorems
3.2
and
3.3

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Because in Algorithm
1

\displaystyle\eta_{i}

\displaystyle:=\tr([\widehat{P}^{(i)}]^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)}))-\tr([P^{(i)}]^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)}))

\displaystyle\hphantom{:}=\|\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})\|_{\tr}-\tr([P^{(i)}]^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)}))\geq 0

(C.1)

by Lemma
B.11
, we get
f(P^{(i+1)})\geq f(P^{(i)})+\omega\eta_{i}\geq f(P^{(i)})
by
the NPDo Ansatz
,
implying that the sequence
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is monotonically increasing.

Since
f(P)
is assumed differentiable in
P
and
{\rm St}(k,n)
is compact,
the sequence
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is uniformly bounded and hence convergent. This proves item (a).

We now prove item (b). There is a subsequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
that converges to
P_{*}
, i.e.,

\lim_{\mathbb{I}\ni i\to\infty}\|P^{(i)}-P_{*}\|_{\F}=0,

(C.2)

where
\mathbb{I}
is an infinite subset of
\{1,2,\ldots\}
.
It remains to show
\mathscr{H}(P_{*})=P_{*}\Lambda_{*}
and
\Lambda_{*}=P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P_{*})\succeq 0
, or, equivalently,

{\cal R}(\mathscr{H}(P_{*}))\subseteq{\cal R}(P_{*})
and
P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P_{*})\succeq 0
.
Assume, to the contrary, that either
{\cal R}(\mathscr{H}(P_{*}))\not\subseteq{\cal R}(P_{*})
or

P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P_{*})\not\succeq 0
or both. Then, by Lemma
B.11
,

\delta:=\|\mathscr{H}(P_{*})\|_{\tr}-\tr(P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P_{*}))>0.

Let
\omega
be the positive constant in
the NPDo Ansatz
.
Since
\|\mathscr{H}(P)\|_{\tr}
,
\tr(P^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P))
, and
f(P)
are continuous in
P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, it follows from
(
C.2
) that there is an
i_{0}\in\mathbb{I}
such that

\displaystyle\Big|\|\mathscr{H}(P_{*})\|_{\tr}-\|\mathscr{H}(P^{(i_{0})})\|_{\tr}\Big|<\delta/3,

(C.3a)

\displaystyle\Big|\tr((P^{(i_{0})})^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i_{0})}))-\tr(P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P_{*}))\Big|<\delta/3,

(C.3b)

\displaystyle f(P_{*})-\omega\delta/6<f(P^{(i_{0})})\leq f(P_{*}).

(C.3c)

By
the NPDo Ansatz
and using (
C.3
), we have

\displaystyle f(P^{(i_{0}+1)})

\displaystyle\geq f(P^{(i_{0})})+\omega\Big[\big\|\mathscr{H}(P^{(i_{0})})\big\|_{\tr}-\tr((P^{(i_{0})})^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i_{0})}))\Big]

\displaystyle>f(P_{*})-\frac{\omega\delta}{6}+\omega\Big[\|\mathscr{H}(P_{*})\|_{\tr}-\frac{\delta}{3}-\tr(P_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P_{*}))-\frac{\delta}{3}\Big]

\displaystyle=f(P_{*})+\frac{\omega\delta}{6}>f(P_{*}),

contradicting
f(P^{(i)})\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}f(P^{(j)})=f(P_{*})
for all
i
.

To prove item (c), we notice that

\omega\sum_{i=0}^{m}\eta_{i}\leq f(P^{(m+1)})-f(P^{(0)})
for any
m\geq 1
.
By the uniform boundedness of
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
and
\omega>0
is a constant, we conclude that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\Big[\big\|\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})\big\|_{\tr}-\tr\big([P^{(i)}]^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})\big)\Big]<\infty,

(C.4)

because of (
C.1
). In Algorithm
1
,
\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})=\widehat{P}^{(i)}\big(V_{i}\Sigma_{i}V_{i}^{\T}\big)

is a polar decomposition. It follows from Lemma
B.12
(a) that

\sigma_{\min}(\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)}))\big\|\sin\Theta\big({\cal R}(\widehat{P}^{(i)}),{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\big)\big\|_{\F}^{2}\leq 2\eta_{i},

which, combined with (
C.4
) and
{\cal R}(\widehat{P}^{(i)})={\cal R}(P^{(i+1)})
, yield (
3.6a
).
Again by Lemma
B.12
(a), we get

\sigma_{\min}(\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)}))\,\frac{\big\|\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})-P^{(i)}\big([P^{(i)}]^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})\big)\big\|_{\F}^{2}}{\big\|\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})\big\|_{2}^{2}}\leq 2\eta_{i}.

(C.5)

Inequality (
C.5
), combined with (
C.4
)
and
\big\|\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})\big\|_{2}\leq\big\|\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})\big\|_{\F}
,
yield (
3.6b
).

∎

The following lemma is an equivalent restatement of
[
47
, Lemma 4.10]

(see also
[
32
, Proposition 7]
) in the context of a metric space.

Lemma C.1

(
[
47
, Lemma 4.10]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be a metric space with metric
\dist(\cdot,\cdot)
, and let

\{\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
be a sequence in
\mathscr{G}
. If

\boldsymbol{y}_{*}\in\mathscr{G}
is an isolated accumulation point of the sequence such that, for every subsequence
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}

converging to
\boldsymbol{y}_{*}
, there is an infinite subset
\widehat{\mathbb{I}}\subseteq\mathbb{I}
satisfying

\dist(\boldsymbol{y}_{i},\boldsymbol{y}_{i+1})\to 0
as
\widehat{\mathbb{I}}\ni i\to\infty
,
then the entire sequence
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
converges to
\boldsymbol{y}_{*}
.

In applying this lemma, on the Grassmann manifold

\mathscr{G}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we will use the unitarily invariant metric
\dist_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
in (
A.1
) in appendix
A
, and, on matrix space

\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, we will use
\|X-Y\|_{2}
as the metric.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Let
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
be a subsequence that converges to
P_{*}
. Then
it can be seen that
[
59
, pp.125-127]

0\leq\dist_{2}({\cal R}(P^{(i)}),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\leq\|P^{(i)}-P_{*}\|_{2}\to 0\,\,\mbox{as $\mathbb{I}\ni i\to\infty$},

(C.6)

i.e.,
{\cal R}(P_{*})
is an accumulation point of the sequence
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
.
This proves item (a).

We now prove item (b). Let
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{1}}
be a subsequence that converges to
{\cal R}(P_{*})
.
Since
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{1}}
is a bounded sequence, it has a convergent subsequence

\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{2}}
that converges to
\widehat{P}_{*}
, where
\mathbb{I}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{I}_{1}
. Clearly
{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*})={\cal R}(P_{*})
,
implying

\displaystyle\rank(\mathscr{H}(\widehat{P}_{*}))=k\quad\mbox{by \eqref{eq:full-rank:assume}, and},

(C.7a)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(\widehat{P}_{*})=\widehat{P}_{*}\big[\widehat{P}_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(\widehat{P}_{*})\big]\quad\mbox{by Theorem~\ref{thm:cvg4SCF4NPDo}(b)}.

(C.7b)

Next,
\{P^{(i+1)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{2}}
has a convergent subsequence
\{P^{(i+1)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{3}}
, say
converging to
\widetilde{P}_{*}
, where
\mathbb{I}_{3}\subseteq\mathbb{I}_{2}
. As a result of Theorem
3.2
(a), we have

\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}f(P^{(i)})=\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}f(P^{(i+1)})=\lim_{i\to\infty}f(P^{(i)}),

and, thus,
f(\widehat{P}_{*})=f(\widetilde{P}_{*})
, a fact that we will need later for proving item (c).
Always
\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})=P^{(i+1)}M_{i}
for some
M_{i}
. In fact
M_{i}=Q_{i}^{\T}\Lambda_{i}
,
or, alternatively,
M_{i}=(P^{(i+1)})^{\T}\mathscr{H}(P^{(i)})
. Letting
\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty
yields

\mathscr{H}(\widehat{P}_{*})=\widetilde{P}_{*}M_{*}
where
M_{*}=\widetilde{P}_{*}^{\T}\mathscr{H}(\widehat{P}_{*})
. This together with
(
C.7
) lead to

{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*})={\cal R}(\mathscr{H}(\widehat{P}_{*}))={\cal R}(\widetilde{P}_{*}).

(C.8)

Therefore, as
\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty
, by (
C.6
) we have

\dist_{2}({\cal R}(P^{(i)}),{\cal R}(P^{(i+1)})\leq\dist_{2}({\cal R}(P^{(i)},{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*}))+\dist_{2}({\cal R}(\widetilde{P}_{*}),{\cal R}(P^{(i+1)})\to 0.

Now use Lemma
C.1
to conclude that the entire sequence

\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
converges to
{\cal R}(P_{*})
. This completes the proof of item (b).

In order to use Lemma
C.1
to prove item (b),
we start with a subsequence of
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
that converges to
{\cal R}(P_{*})
, which led to a subsequence of

\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
that converges to
\widehat{P}_{*}
with
{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*})={\cal R}(P_{*})
,
but not knowing whether
\widehat{P}_{*}=P_{*}
or not.
However for proving item (c), we only need to start with a subsequence of
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
that does converge
to
P_{*}
. Let
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{2}}
be any subsequence that converges to
P_{*}
.
The portion of the proof of item (b)
between (
C.7b
) and (
C.8
) inclusive
is valid with
\widehat{P}_{*}
replaced by
P_{*}
.
We claim
\widetilde{P}_{*}=P_{*}
, which follows from the assumption
that
f(P_{*})>f(P)
for any
P\neq P_{*}
and
{\cal R}(P)={\cal R}(P_{*})
because
{\cal R}(\widetilde{P}_{*})={\cal R}(P_{*})
and
f(\widetilde{P}_{*})=f(P_{*})
.
Finally, we invoke Lemma
C.1
to conclude our proof of item (c).
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of Theorems
6.4
and

Proof of Theorem
6.4
.

Because in Algorithm
1

\eta_{i}:=\tr([\widehat{P}^{(i)}]^{\T}H_{i}\widehat{P}^{(i)})-\tr([P^{(i)}]^{\T}H_{i}P^{(i)})\geq 0

(D.1)

by design,
we have
f(P^{(i+1)})\geq f(P^{(i)})+\omega\eta_{i}\geq f(P^{(i)})
by
the NEPv Ansatz
, implying that
the sequence
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is monotonically increasing.

Since
f(P)
is assumed differentiable in
P
and
{\rm St}(k,n)
is compact,
sequence
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
is bounded and hence convergent. This proves item (a).

We now prove item (b). There is a subsequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
that converges to
P_{*}
, i.e.,

\lim_{\mathbb{I}\ni i\to\infty}\|P^{(i)}-P_{*}\|_{\F}=0,

(D.2)

where
\mathbb{I}
is an infinite subset of
\{1,2,\ldots\}
.
It remains to show
H(P_{*})P_{*}=P_{*}\Omega_{*}
and the eigenvalues of
\Omega_{*}
are the
k
largest ones of
H(P_{*})
,
or, equivalently,

\tr(\Omega_{*})=\tr(P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*})=\max_{P\in{\rm St}(k,n)}\tr(P^{\T}H(P_{*})P)=:S_{k}(H(P_{*})),

(D.3)

where
S_{k}(\cdot)
denotes the sum of the first
k
largest eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix.
Assume, to the contrary, that (
D.3
) does not hold, i.e.,

\delta:=S_{k}(H(P_{*}))-\tr(P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*})>0.

Since
S_{k}(H(P))
,
\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)
, and
f(P)
are continuous in
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, it follows from
(
D.2
) that there is an
i_{0}\in\mathbb{I}
such that

\displaystyle\Big|S_{k}(H(P_{*}))-S_{k}(H(P^{(i_{0})}))\Big|<\delta/3,

(D.4a)

\displaystyle\Big|\tr((P^{(i_{0})})^{\T}H(P^{(i_{0})})P^{(i_{0})})-\tr(P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*})\Big|<\delta/3,

(D.4b)

\displaystyle f(P_{*})-\omega\delta/6<f(P^{(i_{0})})\leq f(P_{*}).

(D.4c)

By
the NEPv Ansatz
and using (
D.4
), we have

\displaystyle f(P^{(i_{0}+1)})

\displaystyle\geq f(P^{(i_{0})})+\omega\Big[S_{k}(H(P^{(i_{0})}))-\tr((P^{(i_{0})})^{\T}H(P^{(i_{0})})P^{(i_{0})})\Big]

\displaystyle>f(P_{*})-\frac{\omega\delta}{6}+\omega\Big[S_{k}(H(P_{*}))-\frac{\delta}{3}-\tr(P_{*}^{\T}H(P_{*})P_{*})-\frac{\delta}{3}\Big]

\displaystyle=f(P_{*})+\frac{\omega\delta}{6}>f(P_{*}),

contradicting
f(P^{(i)})\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}f(P^{(j)})=f(P_{*})
for all
i
.

That
P_{*}
is a KKT point if
H(P)

satisfies (
6.6
) and
\mathscr{M}(P_{*})
is symmetric is a consequence of Theorem
6.1
.

To prove item (c), we notice that

\omega\sum_{i=0}^{m}\eta_{i}\leq f(P^{(m+1)})-f(P^{(0)})
for any
m\geq 1
.
By the uniform boundedness of
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
and
\omega>0
is a constant, we conclude that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}<\infty.

(D.5)

Recall
\widehat{P}^{(i)}
is an orthonormal eigenbasis matrix of
H(P^{(i)})
associated with its
k
largest eigenvalues.
It follows from Lemma
B.10
that

\delta_{i}\,\big\|\sin\Theta\big({\cal R}(\widehat{P}^{(i)}),{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\big)\big\|_{\F}^{2}\leq\eta_{i},

which, combined with (
D.5
) and
{\cal R}(\widehat{P}^{(i)})={\cal R}(P^{(i+1)})
, yield (
6.15a
).
Again by Lemma
B.10
, we get

\delta_{i}\,\frac{\big\|H(P^{(i)})P^{(i)}-P^{(i)}\Lambda_{i}\big\|_{\F}^{2}}{\big[\lambda_{1}(H(P^{(i)}))-\lambda_{n}(H(P^{(i)}))\big]^{2}}\leq\eta_{i}.

(D.6)

Inequality (
D.6
), combined with (
D.5
)
and
5
5

5

We ignore the case
\big[\lambda_{1}(H(P^{(i)}))-\lambda_{n}(H(P^{(i)}))\big]=0
because that corresponds
to
H(P^{(i)})=\xi I_{n}
for some
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.

0<\big[\lambda_{1}(H(P^{(i)}))-\lambda_{n}(H(P^{(i)}))\big]\leq 2\|H(P^{(i)})\|_{2}\leq 2\|H(P^{(i)})\|_{\F},

yield (
6.15b
).

∎

Proof of Theorem
6.5
.

Let
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
be a subsequence that converges to
P_{*}
. Then
it can be seen that
[
59
, pp.125-127]

0\leq\dist_{2}({\cal R}(P^{(i)}),{\cal R}(P_{*}))\leq\|P^{(i)}-P_{*}\|_{2}\to 0\,\,\mbox{as $\mathbb{I}\ni i\to\infty$},

(D.7)

i.e.,
{\cal R}(P_{*})
is an accumulation point of sequence
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
,
where metric
\dist_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined in appendix
A
. This proves item (a).

We now prove item (b), let
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{1}}
be a subsequence that converges to
{\cal R}(P_{*})
.
Since
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{1}}
is a bounded sequence, it has a subsequence
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{2}}

that converges to some
\widehat{P}_{*}
where
\mathbb{I}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{I}_{1}
. Clearly,

{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*})={\cal R}(P_{*})
. Consider the subsequence
\{P^{(i+1)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{2}}
, which, as a bounded
sequence in
\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, has a convergent subsequence
\{P^{(i+1)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{3}}
, where

\mathbb{I}_{3}\subseteq\mathbb{I}_{2}
. Let

Z=\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}P^{(i+1)}\in{\rm St}(k,n).

According to

H(P^{(i)})P^{(i+1)}=P^{(i+1)}(Q_{i}^{\T}\Omega_{i}Q_{i})
for
i\in\mathbb{I}_{3}
,
it holds that

H(\widehat{P}_{*})Z=ZM,\quad M=Z^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})Z.

(D.8)

It follows from Theorem
6.4
(a), which says the entire sequence
\{f(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
converges
to
f(P_{*})
, that

f(P_{*})=\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}f(P^{(i)})=\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}f(P^{(i+1)}),

(D.9)

and hence
f(P_{*})=f(\widehat{P}_{*})=f(Z)
.
Since
H(P^{(i)})P^{(i+1)}=P^{(i+1)}(Q_{i}^{\T}\Omega_{i}Q_{i})
and
P^{(i+1)}

associates with the
k
largest eigenvalues of

H(P^{(i)})
, we conclude that
Z
is an orthonormal eigenbasis matrix of
H(\widehat{P}_{*})
associated with its
k

largest eigenvalues.
We claim that
\widehat{P}_{*}
is also one,
because, otherwise, we would have

\delta:=\tr(Z^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})Z)-\tr(\widehat{P}_{*}^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})\widehat{P}_{*})>0.

(D.10)

We claim that this will yield
f(Z)>f(\widehat{P}_{*})
, contradicting
f(P_{*})=f(\widehat{P}_{*})=f(Z)
. To this end, we
notice that

\displaystyle\tr(Z^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})Z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}\tr([P^{(i+1)}]^{\T}H(P^{(i)})P^{(i+1)}),

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}_{*}^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})\widehat{P}_{*})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}\tr([P^{(i)}]^{\T}H(P^{(i)})P^{(i)}),

and

f(\widehat{P}_{*})=\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}f(P^{(i)}),\quad f(Z)=\lim_{\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty}f(P^{(i+1)}).

Hence there is
i_{0}\in\mathbb{I}_{3}
such that

\displaystyle\tr([P^{(i_{0}+1)}]^{\T}H(P^{(i_{0})})P^{(i_{0}+1)})

\displaystyle>\tr(Z^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})Z)-\frac{\delta}{3},

(D.11a)

\displaystyle\tr([P^{(i_{0})}]^{\T}H(P^{(i_{0})})P^{(i_{0})})

\displaystyle<\tr(\widehat{P}_{*}^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})\widehat{P}_{*})+\frac{\delta}{3},

(D.11b)

and

f(\widehat{P}_{*})<f(P^{(i_{0})})+\frac{\omega\delta}{9},\quad f(Z)>f(P^{(i_{0}+1)})-\frac{\omega\delta}{9}.

(D.11c)

Since
P^{(i_{0}+1)}=\widehat{P}^{(i_{0}+1)}Q_{i_{0}}
in the algorithm, it follows from (
D.11a
)
and (
D.11b
) that

\displaystyle\tr([\widehat{P}^{(i_{0}+1)}]^{\T}H(P^{(i_{0})})\widehat{P}^{(i_{0}+1)})

\displaystyle=\tr([P^{(i_{0}+1)}]^{\T}H(P^{(i_{0})})P^{(i_{0}+1)})

\displaystyle>\tr(Z^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})Z)-\frac{\delta}{3}\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:cvg4SCF4NEPv':pf-6'a}})

\displaystyle=\tr(\widehat{P}_{*}^{\T}H(\widehat{P}_{*})\widehat{P}_{*})+\delta-\frac{\delta}{3}\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:cvg4SCF4NEPv':pf-5'}})

\displaystyle>\tr([P^{(i_{0})}]^{\T}H(P^{(i_{0})})P^{(i_{0})})+\frac{\delta}{3}.\qquad(\mbox{by \eqref{eq:cvg4SCF4NEPv':pf-6'b}})

Now use
the NEPv Ansatz
to conclude

f(P^{(i_{0}+1)})\geq f(P^{(i_{0})})+\frac{\omega\delta}{3}.

(D.12)

Next we combine (
D.11c
) and (
D.12
) to get

\displaystyle f(Z)

\displaystyle>f(P^{(i_{0}+1)})-\frac{\omega\delta}{9}

\displaystyle\geq f(P^{(i_{0})})+\frac{\omega\delta}{3}-\frac{\omega\delta}{9}

\displaystyle>f(\widehat{P}_{*})-\frac{\omega\delta}{9}+\frac{\omega\delta}{3}-\frac{\omega\delta}{9}

\displaystyle=f(\widehat{P}_{*})+\frac{\omega\delta}{9},

contradicting
f(P_{*})=f(\widehat{P}_{*})=f(Z)
.
Hence both
{\cal R}(Z)
and
{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*})
are the eigenspaces of
H(\widehat{P}_{*})
associated with its
k
largest eigenvalues.
Because of assumption (
6.16
) and
{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*})={\cal R}(P_{*})
, we conclude that
{\cal R}(Z)={\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*})={\cal R}(P_{*})
.
Therefore, as
\mathbb{I}_{3}\ni i\to\infty
, we have

\dist_{2}({\cal R}(P^{(i)}),{\cal R}(P^{(i+1)})\leq\dist_{2}({\cal R}(P^{(i)},{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*}))+\dist_{2}({\cal R}(Z),{\cal R}(P^{(i+1)})\to 0.

Now use Lemma
C.1
to conclude that the entire sequence

\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
converges to
{\cal R}(P_{*})
. This completes the proof of item (b).

In order to use Lemma
C.1
to prove item (b), we start with a subsequence of
\{{\cal R}(P^{(i)})\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
that converges to
{\cal R}(P_{*})
, which led to a subsequence of

\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
that converges to
\widehat{P}_{*}
with
{\cal R}(\widehat{P}_{*})={\cal R}(P_{*})
,
but not knowing whether
\widehat{P}_{*}=P_{*}
or not.
However for proving item (c), we only need to start with a subsequence of
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
that does converge
to
P_{*}
. Let
\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{2}}
be any subsequence that converges to
P_{*}
.
The portion of the proof of item (b) up to
before invoking (
6.16
) is valid with
\widehat{P}_{*}
replaced by
P_{*}
, at which point, we shall invoke
(
6.17
) instead to conclude
{\cal R}(Z)={\cal R}(P_{*})
.
We claim
Z=P_{*}
, which follows from the assumption
that
f(P_{*})>f(P)
for any
P\neq P_{*}
and
{\cal R}(P)={\cal R}(P_{*})
because
{\cal R}(Z)={\cal R}(P_{*})
and
f(Z)=f(P_{*})
.
Finally, we invoke Lemma
C.1
to conclude our proof of item (c).
∎

Appendix E
The
M
-inner Product

The developments we have so far can be extended to the case of the
M
-inner product
\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle_{M}=\boldsymbol{y}^{\T}M\boldsymbol{x}
where
M\succ 0
.
Instead of (
1.1
), we face with,

\max_{P^{\T}MP=I_{k}}f(P).

(E.1)

To proceed, we let the Cholesky decomposition of
M
be
M=R^{\T}R
and set
Z=RP
.
It can be verified that

P^{\T}MP=I_{k}
if and only if
Z^{\T}Z=I_{k}
. Hence
Z=RP
and
P=R^{-1}Z
establish an one-one mapping between
Z\in{\rm St}(k,n)

and
\{P\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}\,:\,P^{\T}MP=I_{k}\}
.
Optimization problem (
E.1
) is equivalently turned into

\max_{Z^{\T}Z=I_{k}}\hat{f}(Z):=f(R^{-1}Z),

(E.2)

which is in the form of (
1.1
). The results we have obtained so far are applicable to (
E.2
).
For best outcomes,
it is suggested to write down related results symbolically in
Z
and then translate them back into ones in original matrix variable
P

of (
E.1
). In what follows, we will outline the basic idea.

By the result in section
2
, the KKT condition of (
E.2
) can be stated as

\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(Z):=\frac{\partial\hat{f}(Z)}{\partial Z}=Z\Lambda\quad\mbox{with}\quad\Lambda^{\T}=\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},\quad Z\in{\rm St}(k,n).

(E.3)

It can be verified that

\frac{\partial\hat{f}(Z)}{\partial Z}=R^{-\T}\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}=:R^{-\T}\mathscr{H}(P).

(E.4)

Combine (
E.3
) and (
E.4
) to yield, after simplifications, the KKT condition of (
E.1
) as

\mathscr{H}(P)=MP\Lambda\quad\mbox{with}\quad\Lambda^{\T}=\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},\quad P^{\T}MP=I_{k}.

(E.5)

In principle, an NPDo approach can be established by translating what we have in Part I for solving (
E.3
) for
Z

to solving (
E.5
) for
P
.

As for the NEPv approach in Part II, we can create a counterpart as well. Suppose we have a symmetric matrix-valued function
\widehat{H}(Z)

for optimization problem (
E.2
)
such that

\widehat{H}(Z)Z-\widehat{\mathscr{H}}(Z)=Z\widehat{\mathscr{M}}(Z),

(E.6)

which ensures the equivalency between the KKT condition (
E.3
) and NEPv

\widehat{H}(Z)Z=Z\Omega,\quad\Omega^{\T}=\Omega,\quad Z\in{\rm St}(k,n),

(E.7)

according to Theorem
6.1
.
Plug in
Z=RP
to (
E.6
) and use (
E.4
) to get

\widehat{H}(RP)RP-R^{-\T}\mathscr{H}(P)=RP\mathscr{M}(RP),

and hence

\underbrace{R^{\T}\widehat{H}(RP)R}_{=:H(P)}\,P-\mathscr{H}(P)=MP\,\underbrace{\widehat{\mathscr{M}}(RP)}_{=:\mathscr{M}(P)},

(E.8)

a condition that ensures the equivalency between the KKT condition (
E.5
) and generalized NEPv

H(P)P=MP\Omega,\quad\Omega^{\T}=\Omega,\quad P^{\T}MP=I_{k}.

(E.9)

Theorem E.1
.

Let
H(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a symmetric matrix-valued function on
{\rm St}(k,n)
, satisfying

H(P)P-\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}=MP\,\mathscr{M}(P)\quad\mbox{for $P\in{\rm St}(k,n)$},

(E.10)

where
\mathscr{M}(P)\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is some matrix-valued function.

P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
is a solution to the KKT condition (
E.5
) if and only if
it is a solution to NEPv (
E.9
)
and
\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric.

Proof.

If
P
is a solution to the KKT condition (
E.5
).
Then, by (
E.10
),

H(P)P=MP\Lambda+MP\mathscr{M}(P)=MP(\Lambda+\mathscr{M}(P))=:MP\Omega,

where
\Omega=\Lambda+\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric because alternatively
\Omega=P^{\T}H(P)P
which is symmetric, and hence
\mathscr{M}(P)=\Omega-\Lambda
is also symmetric.
On the other hand, if
P
is a solution to NEPv (
E.9
) such that
\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric, then again
by (
E.10
)

\mathscr{H}(P)=MP\Omega-MP\mathscr{M}(P)=MP\big(\Omega-\mathscr{M}(P)\big)=:MP\Lambda,

where
\Lambda=\Omega-\mathscr{M}(P)
is symmetric because
\mathscr{M}(P)
is assumed symmetric.
∎

Consider, for example, the
\Theta
TR problem but with constraint
P^{\T}MP=I_{k}
instead, for which

f(P)=\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}},\quad\hat{f}(Z)=\frac{\tr(Z^{\T}\widetilde{A}Z+Z^{\T}\widetilde{D})}{[\tr(Z^{\T}\widetilde{B}Z)]^{\theta}},

where
\widetilde{A}=R^{-\T}AR^{-1}
,
\widetilde{B}=R^{-\T}BR^{-1}
, and
\widetilde{D}=R^{-\T}D
. According to the discussion
at the beginning of section
7
,
\widehat{H}(Z)
to use in (
E.7
) is

\widehat{H}(Z)=\frac{2}{[\tr(Z^{\T}\widetilde{B}Z)]^{\theta}}\left(\widetilde{A}+\frac{\widetilde{D}Z^{\T}+Z\widetilde{D}^{\T}}{2}-\theta\,\frac{\tr(Z^{\T}\widetilde{A}Z+Z^{\T}\widetilde{D})}{\tr(Z^{\T}\widetilde{B}Z)}\,\widetilde{B}\right)

Finally,
H(P)
, as defined in (
E.8
), to use in (
E.9
) is given by

H(P)=\frac{2}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}}\left(A+\frac{DP^{\T}M+MPD^{\T}}{2}-\theta\,\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)}{\tr(P^{\T}BP)}\,B\right),

for which

H(P)P-\mathscr{H}(P)=MP\left(\frac{1}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}}\,{D^{\T}P}\right),

and hence any solution to the resulting NEPv (
E.9
) such that
D^{\T}P
is symmetric is a KKT
point of
\Theta
TR with constraint
P^{\T}MP=I_{k}
and vice versa.

Appendix F
Proof of Inequality (
6.11
)

In this appendix, we will refine the argument in
[
67
]
that led to
[
67
, Theorem 2.2]
. Let

g(P)=\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}},\quad f(P)=\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}}=g(P)+\frac{\tr(P^{\T}D)}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}},

and recall

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(P):=\frac{\partial f(P)}{\partial P}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}}\left(A+\frac{D}{2}-\theta\,\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)}{\tr(P^{\T}BP)}\,B\right),

\displaystyle H(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{[\tr(P^{\T}BP)]^{\theta}}\left(A+\frac{DP^{\T}+PD^{\T}}{2}-\theta\,\frac{\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)}{\tr(P^{\T}BP)}\,B\right).

Throughout this section,
0\leq\theta\leq 1
,
B\succeq 0
and
\rank(B)>n-k
which ensures
\tr(P^{\T}BP)\geq s_{k}(B)>0
for any
P\in{\rm St}(k,n)
.

The next lemma
is a refinement of
[
67
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Lemma F.1
.

For
P,\,\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, let

a=\tr(P^{\T}AP),\,\,d=\tr(P^{\T}D),\,\,b=\tr(P^{\T}BP),\,\,\hat{b}=\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}B\widehat{P}).

If

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})\geq\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)+\eta,

(F.1)

then

f(P)+h+\frac{b^{\theta}}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}\eta\leq g(\widehat{P})+\frac{\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})}{[\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}B\widehat{P})]^{\theta}},

(F.2)

where

h=\frac{a+d}{\hat{b}^{\theta}b}\Big[(1-\theta)b+\theta\hat{b}-b^{1-\theta}\hat{b}^{\theta}\Big].

(F.3)

Proof.

It can be verified that

\tr(P^{\T}H(P)P)=2(1-\theta)f(P).

Let
\hat{a}=\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}A\widehat{P})
.
By assumption (
F.1
), we have

\displaystyle\eta+2(1-\theta)f(P)

\displaystyle\leq\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}H(P)\widehat{P})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{b^{\theta}}[\hat{a}+\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})-\theta f_{1}(P)\hat{b}],

\displaystyle b^{\theta}\eta+(1-\theta)f(P)b^{\theta}

\displaystyle\leq\hat{a}+\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})-\theta f_{1}(P)\hat{b},

\displaystyle\frac{b^{\theta}}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}\eta+(1-\theta)f(P)\frac{b^{\theta}}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}

\displaystyle\leq g(\widehat{P})+\frac{\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}-\theta f(P)\frac{\hat{b}^{1-\theta}}{b^{1-\theta}},

implying

g(\widehat{P})+\frac{\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}\geq f(P)+h+\frac{b^{\theta}}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}\eta,

where

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=(1-\theta)f(P)\frac{b^{\theta}}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}+\theta f(P)\frac{\hat{b}^{1-\theta}}{b^{1-\theta}}-f(P)

\displaystyle=(1-\theta)\frac{a+d}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}+\theta\,\frac{a+d}{b}\,\hat{b}^{1-\theta}-\frac{a+d}{b^{\theta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{a+d}{\hat{b}^{\theta}b}\Big[(1-\theta)b+\theta\hat{b}-b^{1-\theta}\hat{b}^{\theta}\Big].

This proves inequality (
F.2
), and it is strict if inequality (
F.1
) is strict.
∎

The next theorem
is a refinement of
[
67
, Theorem 2.2]
.

Theorem F.1
.

For
P,\widehat{P}\in{\rm St}(k,n)
, suppose either
\theta\in\{0,1\}
or
\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)\geq 0
, and let

\widetilde{P}=\widehat{P}Q
where
Q\in{\rm St}(k,k)
is an orthogonal polar factor of
\widehat{P}^{\T}D
.
If (
F.1
) holds, then

\frac{b^{\theta}}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}\eta+f(P)\leq g(\widehat{P})+\frac{\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})}{[\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}B\widehat{P})]^{\theta}}

(F.4)

yielding

\displaystyle f(\widetilde{P})

\displaystyle\geq f(P)+\frac{b^{\theta}}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}\eta+\frac{\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}-\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})}{\hat{b}^{\theta}}

(F.5)

\displaystyle\geq f(P)+\omega\,\eta+[S_{k}(B)]^{-\theta}\,\Big[\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}-\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})\Big],

(F.6)

where

\omega=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{s_{k}(B)}{S_{k}(B)}\right)^{\theta},\quad&\mbox{if $\eta\geq 0$},\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{S_{k}(B)}{s_{k}(B)}\right)^{\theta},\quad&\mbox{if $\eta<0$},\end{cases}

s_{k}(B)
and
S_{k}(B)
are the sum of the
k
smallest eigenvalues and that of the
k
largest eigenvalues of
B
, respectively.

Proof.

In Lemma
F.1
, we note
h\equiv 0

in the case
\theta\in\{0,1\}
, and
h\geq 0
in the case
a+d=\tr(P^{\T}AP+P^{\T}D)\geq 0
,
and hence we have (
F.4
) which yields (
F.5
) upon noticing

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}A\widehat{P})=\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}A\widetilde{P})
,

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}B\widehat{P})=\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}B\widetilde{P})
, and writing

\displaystyle\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}-\Big[\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}-\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})\Big]

\displaystyle=\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}D)-\Big[\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}-\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})\Big],

where we have used
\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}=\tr(\widetilde{P}^{\T}D)
.
Note that
\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}-\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})\geq 0
because

\tr(\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P})\leq\|\widehat{P}^{\T}DP^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{\tr}\leq\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}\|P^{\T}\widehat{P}\|_{2}\leq\|\widehat{P}^{\T}D\|_{\tr}.

Finally use
0<s_{k}(B)\leq b\leq S_{k}(B)

and
0<s_{k}(B)\leq\hat{b}\leq S_{k}(B)
to claim (
F.6
) from
(
F.5
).
∎
